(1) KronQ introduces bidirectional incoherence processing, extending the existing input-side random rotation to the output dimension using the gradient covariance, reducing weight magnitude variance across both input and output dimensions. (2) KronQ derives a new sensitivity metric for inter-layer mixed-precision allocation, driven by the gradient and activation Hessian traces. Notably, in the case of 2-bit weight-only quantization on LLaMA-3-70B, while GPTQ and GPTAQ diverge or produce degenerate quantizations (>2000 perplexity on WikiText-2), KronQ achieves 7.93 perplexity.

</details>


### [106] [Provably Optimal Learning Algorithms for Assistance Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08012)
*Nivasini Ananthakrishnan,Mark Bedaywi,Michael I. Jordan,Stuart Russell,Nika Haghtalab*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides the first provably efficient learning algorithms for repeated assistance games, achieving a (1-1/e)-approximate assistance regret rate of Õ(T^{3/4}) with decentralized algorithms, and an optimal Õ(T^{1/2}) rate in a pseudo-decentralized setting with shared randomness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between assistance games (where an informed human and uninformed assistant cooperate) and practical online learning, by providing the first provably efficient algorithms with formal regret guarantees.

Method: Introduces assistance regret (gap between cumulative utility and optimal joint policies in hindsight). Presents decentralized algorithms using no-regret learning; the assistant can use any no-regret algorithm. For the pseudo-decentralized setting, algorithms leverage a shared random string.

Result: Achieves (1-1/e)-approximate assistance regret of Õ(T^{3/4}) with polynomial runtime. Proves that better than (1-1/e) approximation is computationally intractable. Achieves optimal Õ(T^{1/2}) rate in pseudo-decentralized setting.

Conclusion: First provably efficient learning for repeated assistance games; establishes fundamental computational limits (inapproximability beyond 1-1/e) and provides near-optimal algorithms.

Abstract: This paper studies an online variant of the assistance games framework, where an informed agent and an uninformed agent repeatedly interact over $T$ timesteps to optimize a common reward function. While the informed agent (the human) observes a latent state of the world, the uninformed agent (the assistant) observes only the human's actions. We provide the first provably efficient learning algorithms for repeated assistance games. We introduce the notion of assistance regret: the gap between the cumulative utility of interactions and that of the optimal joint policies in hindsight, which map latent states to action pairs. We present decentralized algorithms for both the human and the assistant that achieve a $(1-1/e)$-approximate assistance regret rate of $\widetilde{O}(T^{3/4})$, with runtime polynomial in the size of the action and state spaces. These algorithms are general; in particular, they accommodate any no-regret algorithm for the assistant. We prove that achieving a regret approximation factor better than $(1-1/e)$ is computationally intractable. Furthermore, we demonstrate how these generic no-regret algorithms can be tailored to a pseudo-decentralized setting -- using a shared random string -- to achieve a rate of $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/2})$, optimal up to logarithmic factors.

</details>


### [107] [Collate: Collaborative Neural Network Learning for Latency-Critical Edge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08013)
*Shuo Huai,Di Liu,Hao Kong,Xiangzhong Luo,Weichen Liu,Ravi Subramaniam,Christian Makaya,Qian Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Collate is a federated learning framework that collaboratively learns heterogeneous models to meet latency constraints of multiple edge systems, using dynamic zeroizing-recovering for model architecture adjustment and proto-corrected federated aggregation, improving accuracy by 1.96-3.09% with no extra training overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing FL optimizations focus on improving training efficiency but fail to speed up inference under latency constraints, especially when deploying FL in heterogeneous real-time edge systems.

Method: A dynamic zeroizing-recovering method adjusts each local model architecture for high accuracy under its latency constraint, and a proto-corrected federated aggregation scheme aggregates heterogeneous local models in a single training process.

Result: Extended models improve accuracy by 1.96% on average, shrunk models improve accuracy by 3.09% on average, with almost no extra training overhead.

Conclusion: Collate effectively addresses inference latency constraints in heterogeneous edge systems while maintaining high accuracy and training efficiency.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) empowers multiple clients to collaboratively learn a model, enlarging the training data of each client for high accuracy while protecting data privacy. However, when deploying FL in real-time edge systems, the heterogeneity of devices among systems has a severe impact on the performance of the inferred model. Existing optimizations on FL focus on improving the training efficiency but fail to speed up inference, especially when there is a latency constraint. In this work, we propose Collate, a novel training framework that collaboratively learns heterogeneous models to meet the latency constraints of multiple edge systems simultaneously. We design a dynamic zeroizing-recovering method to adjust each local model architecture for high accuracy under its latency constraint. A proto-corrected federated aggregation scheme is also introduced to aggregate all heterogeneous local models, satisfying the latency constraint of different systems with only one training process and maintaining high accuracy. Extensive experiments indicate that, compared to state-of-the-art methods and under a latency constraint, our extended models can improve the accuracy by 1.96% on average, and our shrunk models can also obtain a 3.09% accuracy improvement on average, with almost no extra training overhead. The related codes and data will be available at https://github.com/ntuliuteam/Collate

</details>


### [108] [PGD-NO: A Neural Operator with Precomputed Geometry Decomposition for 3D Million-scale Physics Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08025)
*Weiheng Zhong,Jing Bi,Victor Oancea,Hadi Meidani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PGD-NO is a neural operator that uses Precomputed Geometry Decomposition to overcome memory constraints in neural PDE solvers, enabling processing of meshes with over 10 million nodes by offloading geometric encoding to a deterministic pre-computation phase and achieving linear memory scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural PDE solvers suffer from high memory consumption and a single-node bottleneck where mesh resolution is limited by VRAM of one compute unit, preventing large-scale, high-fidelity simulations.

Method: PGD-NO relocates geometric encoding overhead to a deterministic pre-computation phase using an iterative geometry decomposition algorithm to extract geometry tokens, decoupling feature extraction from solution querying, enabling linear memory scalability.

Result: Achieves competitive predictive accuracy across diverse industrial benchmarks, handles meshes exceeding 10 million nodes where existing architectures fail, and provides intrinsic interpretability through attention mechanisms.

Conclusion: PGD-NO effectively overcomes traditional mesh-size constraints and offers a robust, efficient solution for large-scale, high-fidelity industrial design applications.

Abstract: While neural PDE solvers have demonstrated significant potential for accelerating engineering simulations, existing architectures remain constrained by high memory consumption and the single node bottleneck, where the maximum processable mesh resolution is strictly limited by the VRAM of a single compute unit. To address these challenges, we propose PGD-NO, a neural operator with Precomputed Geometry Decomposition, that relocates the computational overhead of geometric encoding to a deterministic pre-computation phase. By utilizing an iterative geometry decomposition algorithm to extract geometry tokens, our model decouples feature extraction from solution querying. This architecture enables linear memory scalability, allowing high fidelity learning on meshes exceeding 10 million nodes, a scale where existing architectures typically encounter memory exhaustion. PGD-NO demonstrates competitive predictive accuracy across diverse industrial benchmarks and provides intrinsic interpretability through attention mechanisms. By effectively overcoming traditional mesh-size constraints, PGD-NO offers a robust and efficient solution for the next generation of large-scale, high-fidelity industrial design applications.

</details>


### [109] [Rethinking Small VLM Quantization: From Component-Wise Analysis to Hardware-Aware Edge Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08029)
*Hyeju Shin,Chorwon Kim,Ryangsoo Kim,Hark Yoo,Jaein Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper systematically evaluates component-wise quantization of sub-3B vision language models on Jetson platforms, validating five hypotheses across six quantization configurations. Key findings include: MoE backbones handle INT4 better than dense backbones; SigLIP encoders show deployment-specific latency issues on Jetson Ampere; INT4 quantization reduces VRAM but increases latency due to dequantization overhead; composite quantization errors are largely additive except along modality-alignment paths; and energy efficiency varies significantly across platforms due to memory bandwidth constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The deployment of on-device multimodal intelligence using sub-3B vision language models is accelerating, but a detailed understanding of component-wise quantization bottlenecks remains lacking, hindering optimal deployment.

Method: A systematic evaluation framework empirically validating five hypotheses across six quantization configurations on Jetson Orin NX and AGX platforms, with separate analysis of vision encoder, projector, and LLM backbone components.

Result: (1) Quantization sensitivity depends on structural paradigm (MoE vs. dense) more than scale; (2) SigLIP encoders show disproportionate INT8 latency on Jetson Ampere due to kernel-hardware interaction; (3) INT4 quantization reduces VRAM but slows token generation from dequantization overhead; (4) Composite quantization errors are mostly additive except along modality-alignment path; (5) Intelligence-per-joule varies across platforms due to memory bandwidth constraints.

Conclusion: Component-wise quantization behavior in VLMs is governed by architecture-specific interactions (MoE/dense paradigm, encoder-hardware coupling) and deployment constraints (memory bandwidth, dequantization overhead), requiring platform-aware quantization strategies rather than simple scale-based heuristics.

Abstract: The emergence of vision language models with fewer than 3 billion parameters has accelerated the implementation of on-device multimodal intelligence. However, a detailed understanding of component-wise quantization remains a bottleneck for optimal deployment. This paper presents a systematic evaluation framework for empirically validating five hypotheses across six quantization configurations on the Jetson Orin NX and AGX. By separating the vision encoder, projector, and large language model backbone yields the following results: (1) Quantization sensitivity is governed by the structural paradigm (MoE vs. dense) rather than scale alone, with MoE backbones mitigating INT4 noise where dense backbones degrade; (2) SigLIP encoders incur disproportionate INT8 latency on Jetson Ampere--a deployment-specific encoder-kernel-hardware interaction, not a SigLIP flaw; (3) Although INT4 quantization of LLMs greatly reduces VRAM consumption, it also causes slower token generation due to dequantization overhead; (4) Composite quantization errors are largely additive, except along the modality-alignment path, which is architecture-dependent; (5) The intelligence-per-joule profile varies significantly across platforms owing to memory bandwidth constraints.

</details>


### [110] [What to Keep, What to Forget: A Rate--Distortion View of Memory Compaction in LLMs and Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08032)
*Ashwin Gerard Colaco,Nada Lahjouji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper unifies four separate research areas (KV cache management, prompt compression, architectural state bounding, and agent memory consolidation) under a single rate-distortion framework for context compaction, proposes a seven-axis taxonomy, identifies common failure patterns (attention magnitude/recency as compaction signals that discard needed information before query is known), and highlights the lack of multi-turn compaction benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory consumption in LLMs and LLM-based agents is growing rapidly across multiple layers (KV cache, prompts, recurrent state, agent session history), with four largely isolated research communities each addressing compaction independently. The paper aims to unify these efforts under a single principled framework to enable cross-layer transfer of mechanisms and systematic evaluation.

Method: The authors formalize a single rate-distortion compaction objective with a layer-agnostic lower bound, construct a seven-axis taxonomy to classify methods uniformly across the stack, and use this lens to transfer KV cache management techniques to agent long-term memory. They also conduct a small reference experiment and propose a benchmark design.

Result: Two cross-layer patterns emerge: (1) compaction signals universally rely on attention magnitude or recency, which fails identically everywhere by discarding query-needed information prematurely; (2) repeated (multi-turn) compaction is virtually unmeasured, with no benchmark holding a budget axis constant across all layers simultaneously.

Conclusion: The paper proposes compaction-aware design principles, a benchmark for multi-turn compaction, and maps open problems, advocating for cross-layer transfer of compaction mechanisms and unified evaluation.

Abstract: Large language models, and the agents built on them, spend an ever-growing share of their compute and memory on remembering: caching attention keys and values, carrying long prompts, maintaining recurrent state, and storing what happened in previous turns and sessions. Because none of this memory is free, four largely separate research communities have each learned to compact it. They evict or quantize the KV cache, prune or distill prompts, bound architectural state, and consolidate agent memory. We argue that these are instances of one problem: a rate--distortion decision about what context-derived information to retain versus discard, at what fidelity, under a resource budget, so as to preserve downstream task utility. We make this lens precise with a single compaction objective and a layer-agnostic lower bound, use it to build a seven-axis taxonomy that classifies methods from across the stack uniformly, and use it to transfer mechanisms between layers that have never been connected, from serving-stack KV management to agent long-term memory. Two patterns hold across the survey. At every layer the signal that decides what to keep is attention magnitude or recency, and it fails in the same way everywhere, by discarding, before the query is known and with no way to undo it, information the query later needs. And while compression is measured carefully on single-turn long context, the repeated compaction that agents actually perform is almost never measured, and no benchmark holds one budget axis across all the layers at once. We turn both observations into a benchmark proposal, a small reference experiment, and a set of compaction-aware design principles, and we map the open problems.

</details>


### [111] [An exact information theory of generalization phase transitions in Bayesian diffusion models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08041)
*Henry Hunt,Mason Kamb,Surya Ganguli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Bayesian information restricted diffusion (BIRD) models to analyze how diffusion models avoid the curse of dimensionality. It identifies an information-theoretic phase boundary between memorization and generalization, showing that generation operates near this boundary by increasingly restricting information over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how diffusion models learn complex high-dimensional distributions from finite training sets without memorizing them, addressing the fundamental mystery of how they circumvent the curse of dimensionality.

Method: Introduces analytically tractable Bayesian information restricted diffusion (BIRD) models where each pixel observes restricted information about noisy data, and time-reverses diffusion by Bayesian posterior inference. Extends spatially local information restriction models and compares to trained diffusion models (UNets, DiTs) early in training.

Result: Identifies an information-theoretic phase boundary: memorization occurs when mutual information between restricted noisy observations and training data exceeds log number of training points; generalization occurs otherwise. Experiments confirm the predicted transition location. Both BIRD models and early-training diffusion models track this boundary by increasingly restricting information over time.

Conclusion: Information restriction plays a fundamental role in enabling generative AI to circumvent the curse of dimensionality, with generation operating near the edge of memorization.

Abstract: How diffusion models circumvent the curse of dimensionality to learn complex distributions over high dimensional spaces from a finite training set, instead of memorizing it, remains a fundamental mystery. To address this, we introduce analytically tractable Bayesian information restricted diffusion (BIRD) models, in which each pixel observes restricted information about noisy data. A BIRD model time-reverses diffusion by inferring which past training sample produced its current restricted observation using the Bayesian posterior. This model class generalizes existing analytical diffusion models that use spatially local information restriction. We show that spatially local BIRD models closely approximate trained diffusion models \textit{early in training}, across different architectures such as UNets and DiTs. Under minimal assumptions on the data distribution, we identify an information-theoretic phase boundary between memorization and generalization in the joint space of amount of training data, time in the reverse generative process, and amount of information restriction: a BIRD model memorizes when the mutual information between its restricted noisy observations and the training data exceeds the log number of training points, and it generalizes otherwise. Experiments across a range of datasets confirm our theoretically predicted location for the transition. We find that generation proceeds near the edge of memorization: both spatially local BIRD models and early-training diffusion models track the memorization-generalization phase boundary by increasingly restricting information over time. Overall, our results reveal a fundamental role for information restriction in generative AI to circumvent the curse of dimensionality.

</details>


### [112] [Who Analyses the Analyser? Self-Validating LLM Hazard Analysis with Constitutional Meta-STPA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08054)
*Samuel Tetteh,Udip Shrestha,Joshua R. Waite,Cody Fleming*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies a blind spot in LLM-assisted safety analysis tools—they never analyze themselves. The authors propose Constitutional Meta-STPA, which applies STPA to the tool itself, deriving 21 Tool Principles and 8 Meta-Safety Principles. They formalize a constitution-marginal coverage operator and show that frontier models can self-derive these principles, while weaker models struggle, indicating the meta-layer is model-limited rather than constitution-limited.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-assisted safety analysis tools analyze various systems but never analyze themselves, creating a blind spot. The authors address the question "who analyzes the analyser?" by applying STPA to the tool itself to ensure its own safety and accountability.

Method: The authors develop Constitutional Meta-STPA, an LLM-assisted STPA tool with a closed-loop architecture. It runs a meta-STPA of AI-assisted safety tools and derives a governance constitution from the loss→hazard→UCA→constraint chain. They formalize a constitution-marginal coverage operator over a principle set P (|P|=29) with a soundness lemma isolating coverage from model and scanner. They test principle recovery using frontier (claude-opus-4.8+claude-sonnet-4) and weaker model ensembles.

Result: A frontier ensemble recovers 18/21 canonical and all 8/8 governance principles from the tool's own design, while a weaker pair recovers only 12/21 and 3/8. The same 8/8 governance principles re-emerge from a second, independently authored tool. This shows the meta layer is model-limited, not constitution-limited.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that LLM-assisted safety tools can be made self-aware through meta-analysis, producing verifiable governance constitutions. However, the quality of this self-analysis depends on the capability of the underlying model, not the constitution design itself.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly trusted to draft the artifacts of safety analysis such as, losses, hazards, Unsafe Control Actions (UCAs), and safety constraints, inside rigorous processes such as Systems-Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA). Yet a blind spot runs through this fast-growing literature: every system gets analysed except the LLM-assisted tool doing the analysing, which is itself a safety-relevant system that can hallucinate standards, emit unverifiable constraints, and leave no audit trail from prompt to artifact. We take seriously the question the field has skipped -- {who analyses the analyser?} and answer it by turning STPA on the tool itself. We present \{Constitutional Meta-STPA}, an LLM-assisted STPA tool built around a closed loop: the tool runs a {meta-STPA} of the class of AI-assisted safety tools and {derives} rather than asserts, its governance constitution from the resulting loss$\to$hazard$\to$UCA$\to$constraint chain, yielding a published constitution of $21$ Tool Principles and $8$ Meta-Safety Principles, each bound to a code enforcement point. We formalise the measured object as a constitution-marginal coverage operator over a principle set $P$ ($|P|{=}29$) with a soundness lemma that isolates coverage from model and scanner, and report four findings. {(i)~Self-derivation:} a frontier ensemble ({claude-opus-4.8}${+}${claude-sonnet-4}) recovers $18/21$ canonical and all $8/8$ governance principles from the tool's own design, while a weaker pair recovers $12/21$ and $3/8$, so the meta layer is model-limited, not constitution-limited, and the same $8/8$ re-emerge from a second, independently authored tool.

</details>


### [113] [Reinforcing the Generation Order of Multimodal Masked Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08056)
*Yidong Ouyang,Zhe Wang,Sourav Bhabesh,Dmitriy Bespalov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a learnable control module trained via GRPO to optimize generation order in Diffusion Language Models, improving text-to-image alignment by 4.08% on GenEval and multimodal understanding by 4.85% on VLMEvalKit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While adaptive token generation ordering improves DLMs for math and code tasks, model logits alone are insufficient for determining optimal generation sequences in text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding, motivating a learnable approach.

Method: A learnable control module trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is introduced to determine the generation order for DLMs in text-to-image and multimodal tasks.

Result: 4.08% relative improvement on GenEval (text-to-image alignment) and 4.85% relative improvement on VLMEvalKit (multimodal understanding).

Conclusion: Learning the generation order via GRPO-based control substantially improves both text-to-image alignment and multimodal understanding, particularly enhancing fine-grained spatial relationships and multimodal reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) have recently achieved substantial progress in natural language generation tasks. Recent research demonstrates that adaptive token generation ordering can significantly improve performance in mathematical reasoning and code synthesis applications. In this work, we investigate the optimization of generation order for both text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding. We first establish that, unlike structured problems in language generation such as Sudoku puzzles, model logits alone are insufficient for determining optimal generation sequences in text-to-image generation and multimodal understanding. To address this challenge, we introduce a learnable control module trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to determine the generation order. Our results demonstrate that learning this control block substantially improves both text-to-image alignment and multimodal understanding in DLMs. In particular, it enhances the model's ability to capture fine-grained spatial relationships in generated images while also strengthening performance on multimodal reasoning and comprehension tasks. We evaluate our framework on GenEval, an object-focused benchmark for text-to-image alignment, where it achieves 4.08% relative improvements. In addition, experiments on VLMEvalKit confirm 4.85% relative improvements in multimodal understanding, highlighting the broad effectiveness of our approach.

</details>


### [114] [Towards Efficient Large Language Model Serving: A Survey on System-Aware KV Cache Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08057)
*Jiantong Jiang,Peiyu Yang,Rui Zhang,Feng Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper surveys system-aware KV cache infrastructure for LLM serving, organizing existing work into three dimensions: execution/scheduling (temporal), placement/migration (spatial), and representation/retention (structural), while analyzing cross-behavior co-design and future opportunities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM serving systems remain memory-intensive and costly due to the KV cache storing KV tensors during autoregressive decoding, motivating the need for a systematic understanding of KV cache infrastructure design.

Method: The authors conduct a survey from a system behavior perspective, organizing recent work into three dimensions: execution and scheduling (temporal), placement and migration (spatial), and representation and retention (structural). They also analyze cross-behavior co-design affinity and behavior-objective links.

Result: A systematic organization of KV cache designs into temporal, spatial, and structural dimensions, along with identified cross-behavior co-design opportunities.

Conclusion: The survey provides a foundation for understanding and innovating KV cache designs in modern LLM serving infrastructure, highlighting future opportunities in cross-behavior co-design.

Abstract: Despite the rapid advancements of large language models (LLMs), LLM serving systems remain memory-intensive and costly. The key-value (KV) cache, which stores KV tensors during autoregressive decoding, is crucial for enabling low-latency, high-throughput LLM inference serving. In this survey, we focus on system-aware KV infrastructure for serving LLMs (abbreviated as sKis). We revisit recent work from a system behavior perspective, organizing existing efforts into three dimensions: execution and scheduling (temporal), placement and migration (spatial), and representation and retention (structural). Furthermore, we analyze cross-behavior co-design affinity and behavior-objective links, highlighting future opportunities. Our work systematizes a rapidly evolving area, providing a foundation for understanding and innovating KV cache designs in modern LLM serving infrastructure.

</details>


### [115] [When Thinking Hurts: Epistemic Signals in the Reasoning Chains of Visual Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08059)
*Mayank Singal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides the first empirical characterization of answer entropy behavior in thinking-mode VLMs, finding three distinct patterns across models. Thinking chain entropy consistently outperforms answer entropy when chains are present, and an abstention gate can dramatically improve accuracy without additional inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional uncertainty quantification for VLMs targets answer token distribution, but thinking-mode VLMs generate explicit reasoning chains before answering. The paper aims to characterize whether thinking chain entropy provides better uncertainty signals than answer entropy, and to document the behavioral patterns of this new model class.

Method: The authors run four models (Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking, GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking, InternVL3-8B, and a baseline) on identical adversarial POPE samples. They measure answer entropy (ans H) and thinking chain entropy, computing AUROC for uncertainty estimation. A 300-sample VQAv2 pilot and HallusionBench reasoning tasks are used for additional validation. They also analyze structured abstention patterns and design a practical abstention gate.

Result: Three distinct entropy behavior patterns: Qwen shows complete collapse (ans H AUROC=0.492), GLM shows no collapse (0.716), InternVL3 shows selective thinking (chains on 50% of samples). Thinking chain entropy outperforms answer entropy on chain-present subsets (0.647/0.759/0.608 vs 0.492/0.716/0.602). VQAv2 pilot confirms chain entropy (0.680) beats answer entropy (0.595). Abstention gate raises accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage.

Conclusion: Thinking chain signals are more reliable than answer entropy for uncertainty quantification whenever chains are present. The advantage is strong for Qwen and GLM but marginal for InternVL3. Structured abstention affects 12-22% of queries with asymmetry toward absent-object queries, and a no-cost abstention gate can dramatically boost accuracy.

Abstract: Uncertainty quantification for visual language models (VLMs) conventionally targets the answer token distribution. We provide the first three-family empirical characterisation of answer entropy behaviour in thinking-mode VLMs. Running four models on identical POPE adversarial samples, we find three qualitatively distinct patterns: Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking shows complete collapse (ans H AUROC = 0.492); GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking shows no collapse (0.716); and InternVL3-8B shows selective thinking (chains on only 50% of samples, ans H = 0.675 full / 0.602 thinking-only). Across all three thinking-mode models, thinking chain entropy outperforms answer entropy on the subset where chains are generated (0.647, 0.759, 0.608 vs. 0.492, 0.716, 0.602 respectively), suggesting chain signals are the more reliable predictor whenever chains are present. This holds strongly for Qwen and GLM, but with only marginal and statistically unreliable advantage for InternVL3 (n_FP = 17). A 300-sample VQAv2 pilot confirms chain entropy (0.680) outperforms answer entropy (0.595) on VQAv2 questions, with the gap largest for free-form answers (0.733 vs. 0.467). On harder reasoning tasks (HallusionBench) both Qwen models show moderate signal (approx. 0.64), consistent with incomplete pre-commitment on difficult questions. We additionally document structured abstention affecting 12-22% of queries with asymmetry toward absent-object queries, and a practical abstention gate raising accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage with no additional inference cost.

</details>


### [116] [Cross-Modal Generative Framework for Signal Translation from Fetal-Maternal Electrocardiograms to Fetal Doppler Waveforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08073)
*Tongli Su,Alireza Rafiei,Marly van Assen,Reza Sameni,Gari D. Clifford,Faezeh Marzbanrad,Nasim Katebi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cross-modal generative framework using dilated convolutions and cross-modal attention synthesizes Doppler ultrasound envelopes from dual-lead (maternal+fetal) ECG, achieving PSD MSE of 49.9 dB² (51% lower than baseline) and heart-rate error of 4.71 bpm, enabling analysis of recoverable vs. unrecoverable mechanical components in fetal circulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the relative contributions of electrical (fECG) versus mechanical (Doppler) factors in fetal circulation by determining which Doppler components are recoverable from ECG, and to leverage maternal-fetal cardiac coupling for improved fetal hemodynamic assessment.

Method: Cross-modal generative framework combining dilated convolutions with cross-modal attention (to selectively incorporate maternal ECG) and self-attention (to capture long-range temporal dependencies), trained on 885 synchronized fetal/maternal ECG and Doppler envelope segments from 39 pregnancies.

Result: PSD MSE of 49.9±15.8 dB² (51% lower than baseline), heart-rate error of 4.71±0.77 bpm (1.5% better than baseline); cross-modal attention yields 39% PSD MSE reduction over naive dual-channel concatenation.

Conclusion: The framework advances computational modeling of maternal-fetal cardiovascular dynamics by synthesizing Doppler from ECG, and enables quantification of purely mechanical contributions (unrecoverable from electrical recordings) for more comprehensive fetal assessment.

Abstract: Fetal electrocardiogram (fECG) and Doppler ultrasound provide complementary views of fetal cardiovascular function: fECG captures electrical activity while Doppler reflects mechanical hemodynamics shaped by factors such as placental resistance and vascular compliance. Understanding the recoverable and unrecoverable Doppler components through reconstruction from fECG offers insight into the relative contributions of electrical versus mechanical factors in fetal circulation, thereby informing clinical decisions. In addition, clinical evidence of maternal-fetal cardiac coupling suggests that maternal cardiovascular dynamics may also inform fetal hemodynamics. To computationally model these relationships, we propose a cross-modal generative framework combining dilated convolutions with cross-modal attention to selectively incorporate maternal ECG and self-attention to capture long-range temporal dependencies. Trained on 885 synchronized fetal/maternal ECG and Doppler envelope segments from 39 pregnancies, our model synthesizes Doppler envelopes with power spectral density mean squared error (PSD MSE) of 49.9 +/- 15.8 dB^2 (51% lower than two-channel baseline) and heart-rate error of 4.71 +/- 0.77 bpm (1.5% better than baseline; negligible relative to the 110-160 bpm physiological range). Cross-modal attention yields a 39% PSD MSE reduction over naive dual-channel concatenation, quantifying the contribution of maternal-fetal coupling. Our proposed framework advances computational modeling of the maternal-fetal cardiovascular system by enabling the synthesis of Doppler envelopes from dual-lead ECG. By analysis of both recoverable and residual Doppler components, this approach enables quantification of the purely mechanical contributions to Doppler waveforms -- those not recoverable from electrical recordings -- ultimately facilitating a more comprehensive fetal assessment.

</details>


### [117] [Modular Pretraining Enables Access Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08077)
*Ethan Roland,Murat Cubuktepe,Erick Martinez,Stijn Servaes,Keenan Pepper,Mike Vaiana,Diogo Schwerz de Lucena,Judd Rosenblatt,Addie Foote,Cem Anil,Alex Cloud*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRAM is a pre-training method using gradient-routed auxiliary modules to enable selective capability removal from neural networks at inference time, addressing the dual-use AI dilemma by approximating data-filtered models at lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI developers face a dual-use dilemma where capabilities beneficial for legitimate users (e.g., curing disease) could also be misused (e.g., synthesizing diseases). Access control via separate models per user is prohibitively expensive, motivating a more efficient approach.

Method: GRAM adds auxiliary modules to a neural network during pre-training and selectively updates them to induce specialization. At inference time, ablating a module removes its targeted capability. The method is validated on synthetic and realistic dual-use data (virology, cybersecurity, nuclear physics, specialized code) with Chinchilla-optimal scaling from 50M to 5B parameters.

Result: GRAM effectively disables targeted capabilities while preserving retained ones, resists capability recovery under finetuning better than post-hoc unlearning. Scaling analysis shows the gap between data-filtered and full-data models widens with scale on removed capabilities but stays small on retained ones. GRAM closely tracks data filtering with 5x cost reduction in a 5-profile setting.

Conclusion: GRAM provides a scalable, efficient alternative to training separate models for different trust levels, enabling practical access control for dual-use AI capabilities at substantially reduced computational cost.

Abstract: AI developers face a dual-use dilemma. An AI capability that helps one user cure a disease can help another synthesize one. This dilemma could be resolved with access control, limiting dual-use AI capabilities to trusted deployments with a legitimate need. A gold standard for access control would be to serve separate models with different capabilities to different users. However, training and deploying multiple models is prohibitively expensive. To address this challenge, we propose gradient-routed auxiliary modules (GRAM), a pre-training method that adds modules to a neural network and selectively updates them to induce specialization. Ablating a module at inference time removes its capability from the network, approximating a model trained on filtered data. We evaluate GRAM on synthetic stories and realistic dual-use data spanning virology, cybersecurity, nuclear physics, and specialized code. These experiments show that GRAM disables targeted capabilities while preserving the rest, and resists their recovery under finetuning better than post-hoc unlearning. Most importantly, a Chinchilla-optimal scaling analysis from 50M to 5B parameters shows that the gap between data-filtered and full-data models widens with scale on removed capabilities but stays small on retained ones, and that GRAM closely tracks data filtering. GRAM's training cost is independent of the number of supported capability profiles, yielding a 5x reduction over data filtering in our 5-profile setting.

</details>


### [118] [Deep Learning Method for Stationary Distribution of Reflected Brownian Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08091)
*Jim Dai,Zhanhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a deep learning approach to learn the Laplace transform of high-dimensional reflected Brownian motion (RBM) using the basic adjoint relationship (BAR), achieving near-perfect prediction of tail probabilities in high-dimensional settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stationary distributions of RBMs are important for analyzing high-dimensional stochastic systems, but closed-form solutions exist only for a few special cases, and computing tail probabilities is even more intractable.

Method: A deep learning framework that combines a carefully designed loss function based on the basic adjoint relationship (BAR), a tailored training data sampling procedure, and a specific neural network architecture to learn the Laplace transform of high-dimensional RBMs.

Result: The method achieves near-perfect prediction of tail probabilities on RBM instances with known ground-truth in high-dimensional settings.

Conclusion: The proposed approach demonstrates strong potential as a general tool for analyzing stochastic systems beyond analytically tractable regimes, with code publicly available.

Abstract: The stationary distribution of reflected Brownian motion (RBM) plays an important role in the analysis of high-dimensional stochastic systems, yet closed-form solutions are known only for a few special cases. Computing important performance metrics, such as tail probabilities, is even more intractable, despite their practical relevance. In this paper, we develop a deep learning approach that accurately and efficiently learns the Laplace transform of high-dimensional RBMs based on the basic adjoint relationship (BAR). Our framework combines a careful design of the loss function, training data sampling procedure, and neural network architecture. We evaluate the proposed method on RBM instances with known ground-truth tail probabilities and demonstrate near-perfect prediction in high-dimensional settings, highlighting its potential as a general tool for analyzing stochastic systems beyond analytically tractable regimes. Our code can be found at https://github.com/zhangz73/NN4MGF.

</details>


### [119] [Stochastic Order Learning: An Approach to Rank Estimation Using Noisy Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08103)
*Chaewon Lee,Seon-Ho Lee,Chang-Su Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Stochastic Order Learning (SOL), a framework for rank estimation under label noise that reformulates the problem as stochastic ordering, capturing ordinal label uncertainty through discriminative and stochastic order losses, achieving reliable performance across diverse datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rank estimation under label noise is challenging because ordinal annotations often exhibit structured uncertainty rather than simple label corruption, requiring a fundamentally different approach.

Method: Reformulates rank estimation as a stochastic ordering problem where each instance has multiple plausible ranks. Proposes SOL with two complementary losses: a discriminative loss for instance-centroid interactions and a stochastic order loss for probabilistic ordering relations between instances.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse datasets demonstrate reliable rank estimation under various types and levels of label noise.

Conclusion: SOL effectively captures ordinal label uncertainty and provides a principled framework for rank estimation under noisy ordinal labels.

Abstract: Rank estimation under label noise poses a fundamental challenge, as ordinal annotations often exhibit structured uncertainty rather than simple label corruption. In this paper, we reformulate rank estimation with noisy ordinal labels as a stochastic ordering problem, in which each instance is inherently associated with multiple plausible ranks instead of a single deterministic label. Based on this view, we propose stochastic order learning (SOL), a learning framework that captures ordinal label uncertainty and learns an embedding space through two complementary objectives: a discriminative loss that structures instance--centroid interactions and a stochastic order loss that enforces probabilistic ordering relations between instances. Extensive experiments across diverse datasets demonstrate that SOL enables reliable rank estimation under various types and levels of label noise. The source code is available at https://github.com/cwlee00/SOL.

</details>


### [120] [Vanilla SGD with Momentum Survives Heavy-Tailed Noise: Convergence Analysis without Gradient Clipping or Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08104)
*Ryusei Yamada,Naoki Sato,Hideaki Iiduka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes vanilla SGD (with and without momentum) under heavy-tailed noise without gradient clipping/normalization, showing that convergence rates are inferior to optimal rates achieved by modified variants, supported by experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how vanilla SGD (especially with momentum) performs fundamentally under heavy-tailed noise without any gradient control mechanisms, and to reveal inherent limitations of vanilla methods compared to clipped/normalized variants.

Method: Theoretical convergence analysis of vanilla SGD with momentum for strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex objectives under heavy-tailed noise, without employing gradient clipping or normalization. Experiments on synthetic functions support the theory.

Result: Convergence rates of vanilla SGD with momentum under heavy-tailed noise are inferior to the optimal rates achieved by clipped or normalized variants of SGD.

Conclusion: Vanilla SGD methods have inherent limitations under heavy-tailed noise, and gradient control mechanisms (clipping/normalization) are necessary to achieve optimal convergence rates.

Abstract: Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is a cornerstone of modern optimization. While its performance under heavy-tailed noise is often addressed through specialized modifications such as gradient clipping or normalization, we investigate a more fundamental question: how does vanilla SGD, particularly with momentum, perform in the presence of heavy-tailed noise? In this paper, we refine existing convergence results for vanilla SGD and, more importantly, provide the first comprehensive convergence analysis of vanilla SGD with momentum for strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex objectives, without employing any gradient control mechanisms. Our results demonstrate that the obtained convergence rates are inferior to the optimal rates achieved by clipped or normalized variants of SGD, thereby revealing inherent limitations of vanilla methods under heavy-tailed noise. The theoretical findings are supported by experiments on synthetic functions.

</details>


### [121] [Contrastive Order Learning: A General Framework for Ordinal Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08109)
*Chaewon Lee,BeomJun Shim,Kwang Pyo Choi,Chang-Su Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ConOrd is a contrastive learning framework for ordinal regression that combines contrastive learning with order learning via a novel loss function using soft affinity and disparity weights based on rank differences, achieving SOTA on facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contrastive learning ignores inherent ordering among rank labels, while order learning relies on local margin-based comparisons and fails to capture global ordinal structure. ConOrd addresses both limitations.

Method: ConOrd introduces a contrastive order loss with soft affinity and disparity weights derived from rank differences, enabling fine-grained ordinal relationship modeling across all sample pairs within a batch.

Result: Consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance across facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment tasks.

Conclusion: ConOrd generalizes well across diverse ordinal regression scenarios and outperforms existing methods, with open-source code available.

Abstract: We propose contrastive order learning (ConOrd), a contrastive learning framework for ordinal regression that integrates the strengths of contrastive learning and order learning. While contrastive learning effectively leverages all samples in a batch, it typically ignores the inherent ordering among rank labels. Conversely, order learning explicitly models label ordinality but often relies on local, margin-based comparisons, limiting its ability to capture global ordinal structure. ConOrd addresses these limitations by introducing a contrastive order loss with soft affinity and disparity weights based on rank differences, enabling fine-grained modeling of ordinal relationships across all sample pairs within a batch. Extensive experiments on a range of ordinal regression tasks, including facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment, demonstrate that ConOrd consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance and generalizes well across diverse ordinal regression scenarios. The source code is available at https://github.com/cwlee00/ConOrd.

</details>


### [122] [Workload-Preserving Differentially Private Synthetic Data for Causal Inference via Maximum-Entropy Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08122)
*Amir Asiaee,Kaveh Aryan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces causal workloads—DP query sets designed around orthogonal moments for doubly robust causal estimators—to preserve causal estimands like ATE in differentially private synthetic data. It presents Causal-AIM for adaptive workload selection and noise-aware multiple imputation for valid confidence intervals, showing causal workloads excel under strict privacy budgets while generic workloads favor point accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generic differentially private synthetic data methods preserve distributional fidelity but fail to adequately preserve causal estimands (e.g., ATE), which depend on treatment-arm balance and outcome moments not captured by generic marginals.

Method: The authors propose causal workloads—DP query sets based on orthogonal moments used by doubly robust causal estimators—and introduce Causal-AIM, an adaptive workload selector. They also develop noise-aware multiple imputation (NA+MI) for confidence intervals from DP synthetic data, and decompose ATE error into sampling, privacy, workload-approximation, Monte Carlo, and calibration terms.

Result: Causal workloads are most effective under strict privacy budgets and for calibrated uncertainty; generic workloads retain a point RMSE advantage as privacy relaxes. The released workload supports ATE, ATT, and subgroup analyses without additional privacy spending.

Conclusion: There is a fundamental tradeoff: distributional fidelity aids point accuracy, but valid causal inference requires preserving causal moments and propagating DP noise rather than treating synthetic rows as real.

Abstract: Workload-based differentially private (DP) synthetic data methods privately measure aggregate queries and post-process the noisy answers into synthetic records. Generic workloads can achieve strong distributional fidelity, but causal estimands such as the average treatment effect (ATE) depend on treatment-arm balance and outcome moments that generic marginals need not preserve. We propose causal workloads: DP query sets designed around the orthogonal moments used by doubly robust causal estimators. The released workload can be used directly by stable moment-map estimators or reconstructed by maximum-entropy calibration into reusable synthetic data; our theory decomposes ATE error into sampling, privacy, workload-approximation, Monte Carlo, and calibration terms. We also introduce Causal-AIM, an adaptive workload selector, and a noise-aware multiple-imputation (NA+MI) procedure for confidence intervals from DP synthetic data. Because the workload is released once, the same DP synthetic table can support ATE, ATT, and subgroup analyses without additional privacy spending. Empirically, causal workloads are most useful at strict privacy budgets and for calibrated uncertainty, while generic workloads often retain an advantage for point RMSE as privacy relaxes. The broader lesson is a tradeoff: distributional fidelity can help point accuracy, but valid causal inference requires preserving causal moments and propagating DP noise rather than treating synthetic rows as real.

</details>


### [123] [DeepPySR -- A Symbolic Regression Framework with Dynamic Pruning, Pareto Selection, and Hierarchical Composition for Real-World Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08150)
*Fuling Chen,Kevin Vinsen,Phillip Melton,Rae-Chi Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeepPySR is a symbolic regression method that addresses high-dimensional inputs, Pareto-front selection, and data irregularities using dynamic variable pruning, exponential Pareto selection, and multi-layer hierarchical composition, outperforming baselines on physics, biomedical, and social science datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symbolic regression offers interpretable glass-box models crucial for clinical medicine and social science, but suffers from three key challenges: high-dimensional inputs, principled Pareto-front formula selection, and data irregularities like multicollinearity and class imbalance.

Method: DeepPySR introduces (1) a dynamic variable-pruning schedule to remove irrelevant features during search, (2) an exponential Pareto selection criterion that eliminates trade-offs between accuracy and complexity, and (3) a multi-layer architecture for hierarchical symbolic composition.

Result: On Feynman physics benchmarks and seven biomedical/social-science datasets, DeepPySR outperforms PySR and baselines: body fat (R²: 0.794 vs. 0.702), heart disease (F1: 0.898 vs. 0.787), student performance (R²: 0.964 vs. 0.948), and Raine BMI (R²: 0.525 vs. 0.370).

Conclusion: DeepPySR produces interpretable formulas aligned with domain risk factors, effectively overcoming key limitations of symbolic regression for high-stakes domains requiring transparency.

Abstract: Symbolic regression (SR) discovers analytical equations from data, yielding glass-box models with directly interpretable formulas, unlike black-box methods that rely on unstable post-hoc tools such as SHAP or LIME. This transparency is crucial in clinical medicine and social science, but SR faces three challenges: high-dimensional inputs, principled selection of Pareto-front formulae, and data irregularities such as multicollinearity and class imbalance. We introduce DeepPySR, which addresses these issues with a dynamic variable-pruning schedule to remove irrelevant features during search, an exponential Pareto selection criterion that eliminates trade-offs between accuracy and complexity, and a multi-layer architecture for hierarchical symbolic composition. On four Feynman physics benchmarks and seven biomedical and social-science datasets, DeepPySR outperforms PySR and baselines on body fat (R$^2$: 0.794 vs.\ 0.702), heart disease (F1: 0.898 vs.\ 0.787), student performance (R$^2$: 0.964 vs.\ 0.948), and Raine BMI (R$^2$: 0.525 vs.\ 0.370), producing interpretable formulas aligned with domain risk factors.

</details>


### [124] [Understanding Layer Patching in Model Size Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08170)
*Sara Kangaslahti,Jonathan Geuter,Nihal V. Nayak,Marco Fumero,Francesco Locatello,David Alvarez-Melis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies student-layer selection for zero-shot model size interpolation via layer patching, showing that sequential patching strategies work well in practice and introducing KLPatch, a greedy KL-divergence-based algorithm that approximately solves the optimal layer selection problem modeled as a shortest-path problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work on boomerang distillation showed that layer patching can interpolate model sizes, but there was no systematic study of how to select which student layers to patch—the choice of layer subset is critical for interpolation quality.

Method: Formulates optimal layer subset selection as an optimization problem, proves it reduces to a shortest-path problem in an acyclic graph, evaluates sequential patching strategies (first-to-last, last-to-first) across model families, and proposes KLPatch, a greedy algorithm based on KL divergence.

Result: Patching strongly shapes interpolation behavior with effects varying across model families; simple sequential strategies (first-to-last or last-to-first) often achieve strong performance; KLPatch improves over last-to-first patching and approximately solves the optimization.

Conclusion: Provides principled understanding of how layer patching affects model size interpolation and offers practical guidance for constructing near-optimal interpolated models without additional training.

Abstract: Zero-shot model size interpolation aims to create new models of intermediate target sizes by combining existing models without additional training. Recent work on boomerang distillation [Kangaslahti et al., 2026] shows that a student language model distilled from a larger teacher can be expanded by iteratively patching its layers, replacing student layers with contiguous blocks of teacher layers to obtain models whose size and performance interpolate between the student and the teacher. In this work, we provide the first systematic study of student-layer selection for model size interpolation. We cast finding the optimal layer subset for each model size as an optimization problem and prove it can be viewed as a shortest-path problem in a certain acyclic graph. In experiments, we show that patching strongly shapes interpolation behavior, with effects that vary substantially across model families. We find that simple sequential strategies--patching either from the first layer to the last or from the last to the first--often achieve surprisingly strong performance in practice. We further introduce KLPatch, a greedy patching algorithm based on KL divergence, which often improves over last-to-first patching and approximately solves the optimization problem. Together, our results provide a principled understanding of how layer patching affects model size interpolation and offer practical guidance for constructing near-optimal interpolated models.

</details>


### [125] [Open-ended Multi-agent Autocurricula via Visual Inspection of Policies with Multi-modal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08193)
*Lorenzo Pantè,Andrea Fanti,Roberto Capobianco*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VIP uses a Video Language Model to directly inspect policy behavior via recorded episode videos for curriculum generation in RL, outperforming text-only and scalar score methods on SMAC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-ended curricula in RL need to assess task difficulty relative to agent's learning progress; prior work used scalar scores or text summaries, but this paper explores directly inspecting policy behavior via recorded episode videos as a richer signal.

Method: VIP (Visual Inspection of Policies) leverages a lightweight VLM (VideoLLaMa2-7B) to process recorded episode videos and provide curriculum recommendations; studied on StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC).

Result: VIP generates more effective curricula than text-only ablation and methods relying on scalar task scores.

Conclusion: Video-based inspection of policy behavior via VLM is a simple yet effective approach for open-ended curriculum design in RL, outperforming text-only and scalar-based alternatives.

Abstract: Open-ended curricula in Reinforcement Learning (RL) aim to train generally-capable agents by identifying tasks that facilitate learning increasingly complex skills. A major challenge when designing such curricula is assessing task difficulty relative to the agent's current learning progress. While previous work has explored using scalar task scores or textual summaries of the agent's behavior, here we study a different approach: directly inspecting policy behavior via recorded episode videos. We introduce a simple yet effective instantiation of this approach which leverages a Video Language Model (VLM) to both process these videos and provide curriculum recommendations, which we call Visual Inspection of Policies (VIP). Since videos can naturally contain any number of controllable agents, we empirically study VIP on the StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC). We show that even with a lightweight and openly accessible VLM (VideoLLaMa2-7B), VIP can use policy videos to generate more effective curricula than both its text-only ablation and methods that rely on scalar task scores.

</details>


### [126] [PIT-SUN: A Deployable Empirical Marginal Transform Framework with Expectation-Consistent Recovery for Regression in Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08202)
*Mingyu Zhao,Zhaohan Li,Zhenxiong Miao,Xu Zhang,Dewei Leng,Yanan Niu,Kun Gai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PIT-SUN is a framework that uses probability-integral-transformed unbiased recovery to estimate original-space conditional expectations for heavy-tailed, zero-inflated targets in recommender systems, achieving expectation consistency without requiring affine inverse transforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MSE loss and direct inverse-transform methods fail on heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, multimodal targets due to gradient instability and expectation inconsistency. Existing conditionally linear recovery methods leave open key design choices for sparse complex marginals.

Method: PIT-SUN uses one empirical marginal table to define a bounded normal-score coordinate, inverse-quantile lookup, variance-controlled recovery base, and drift monitoring, then applies multiplicative SUN (Synthetic Unbiased Normal-score) recovery to estimate original-space expectations instead of directly inverting transformed predictions.

Result: Experiments on synthetic distributions, public benchmarks, large-scale industrial datasets, and online deployment show robust improvements in point accuracy, calibration, and ranking quality with lightweight deployment overhead.

Conclusion: PIT-SUN effectively resolves the scale conflict and expectation inconsistency inherent in target transformation for value-driven recommender systems, providing deployable unbiased recovery for sparse complex marginals.

Abstract: Estimating original-space conditional expectations is central to value-driven recommender systems, including dwell time, GMV, and LTV forecasting. Standard MSE is expectation-consistent in principle, but its gradients become unstable on heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, and multimodal targets, causing mean collapse and tail shrinkage. Target transformation alleviates this scale conflict, yet any useful nonlinear marginal transform loses expectation consistency under direct inversion. This is not an implementation oversight: a direct inverse-transform estimator is universally expectation-consistent only when the inverse transform is affine, which cannot simultaneously provide bounded tail compression. Existing conditionally linear recovery methods restore expectation consistency, but still leave open which coordinate, inverse lookup, recovery base, and deployment monitor should be selected for sparse complex marginals. We propose \textbf{P}robability-\textbf{I}ntegral-\textbf{TranS}formed \textbf{Un}biased recovery (\textbf{PIT-SUN}), a deployable empirical marginal recovery framework. PIT-SUN uses one empirical marginal table to define a bounded normal-score coordinate, its inverse-quantile lookup, a variance-controlled recovery base, and drift monitoring, then applies multiplicative SUN recovery to estimate the original-space expectation instead of directly inverting transformed predictions. Experiments on synthetic distributions, public benchmarks, large-scale industrial datasets, and online deployment show robust improvements in point accuracy, calibration, and ranking quality with lightweight deployment overhead.

</details>


### [127] [RhyMix: A Lightweight Adaptive Multi-Rhythm Network for Long-Term Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08234)
*Sumit Satishrao Shevtekar,Chandresh Kumar Maurya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RhyMix is a lightweight hybrid neural architecture for time series forecasting that uses parallel dual-path modeling (Cyclic Path with seasonal inductive bias + Multi-Scale Temporal Convolutional Network with Channel Attention) and adaptive gating mechanisms to capture multiple simultaneous temporal patterns, achieving state-of-the-art performance on 10/12 datasets with only ~40K parameters and <5ms inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing forecasting architectures rely on single-path temporal modeling (transformers, convolutions, or linear models) that fail to simultaneously capture short-term fluctuations, periodic seasonal cycles, long-term trends, and irregular abrupt changes present in real-world time series.

Method: RhyMix employs a parallel dual-path paradigm: (i) a Cyclic Path with learnable cyclic embeddings for seasonal inductive bias, and (ii) a lightweight Multi-Scale Temporal Convolutional Network with Channel Attention (MSTCN-CA) path using multi-scale depthwise dilated convolutions. Adaptive gating at multiple levels (path gate combining four forecasting heads; hybrid gate balancing two paths) enables dynamic adaptation to input characteristics while maintaining linear complexity.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on 10 out of 12 real-world long-term forecasting benchmarks. The model is lightweight (~40K parameters) with linear complexity and low-latency inference (<5ms), suitable for edge deployment.

Conclusion: The dual-path hybrid design with adaptive gating effectively addresses the limitations of single-path models for complex multi-pattern time series forecasting, offering both high accuracy and computational efficiency for resource-constrained environments.

Abstract: Real-world time series exhibit complex dynamics characterized by multiple simultaneous temporal patterns: short-term fluctuations, periodic seasonal cycles, long-term trends, and irregular abrupt changes. However, many existing forecasting architectures rely on single-path temporal modeling--transformers capture long-range dependencies but smooth local variations, convolutions capture local patterns but have limited receptive fields, and linear models are efficient but cannot capture nonlinear dynamics. To address this, we introduce RhyMix (RHYthm MIXture), a hybrid neural architecture designed around a parallel dual-path modeling paradigm with adaptive gating mechanisms. RhyMix integrates two complementary encoding branches: (i) a Cyclic Path that incorporates explicit seasonal inductive bias through learnable cyclic embeddings, capturing predictable rhythmic patterns; and (ii) a lightweight Multi-Scale Temporal Convolutional Network with Channel Attention Path that employs multi-scale depthwise dilated convolutions to capture temporal dependencies across different receptive fields. A key innovation is the use of adaptive gating at multiple levels: a path gate dynamically combines four specialized forecasting heads (Direct, Trend-Seasonal Decomposition, Local Convolution, and Periodic Fusion) per sample and channel, while a hybrid gate adaptively balances the Cyclic and MSTCN-CA Paths based on input characteristics. This design ensures the model adapts to specific temporal patterns while maintaining linear complexity in sequence length, channels, and prediction horizon. Across extensive benchmarks on 12 real-world datasets for long-term forecasting, RhyMix achieves state-of-the-art performance on 10 of 12 datasets. The model remains lightweight (~40K params) with linear complexity and low-latency inference (<5ms),suitable for resource-constrained edge devices and real-time deployment.

</details>


### [128] [Structure Learning on Clustered Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08238)
*Ryan Thompson,Matt P. Wand,Veerabhadran Baladandayuthapani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new differentiable graph coupling approach extends mixed-effects modeling to DAG structure learning for clustered data, handling cluster-specific variations while recovering a global causal structure with provable convergence and asymptotic consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DAG structure learning methods assume homogeneous populations and cannot handle clustered data where cluster-specific (e.g., patient-specific) variations exist. The paper aims to bridge this gap by introducing a method that estimates a global structure while accounting for local cluster-level effects.

Method: The authors extend the fixed- and random-effects framework from classical mixed models to structure learning. They propose a differentiable graph coupling mechanism to ensure the union of fixed- and random-effects graphs remains acyclic, and provide a provably convergent first-order method with efficient batched updates across clusters.

Result: Identifiability of the model is established, and the approach is shown to recover the true structure asymptotically. On real and synthetic data, the proposal detects dependencies missed by alternative estimators.

Conclusion: The proposed mixed-effects structure learning approach is effective for clustered settings, capturing cluster-specific variations while preserving global causal structure, outperforming alternatives in detecting dependencies.

Abstract: Recent algorithmic advances have made directed acyclic graph (DAG) structure learning scalable for causal discovery. Yet, the currently available techniques assume a completely homogeneous population, precluding their application to clustered data where cluster-specific variations (e.g., patient-specific effects) are common. We address this issue by introducing a new approach that estimates a global structure while accounting for local cluster-level effects. The key idea is to extend the fixed- and random-effects framework of classical mixed models to the structure learning setting. Towards this end, we present a differentiable graph coupling mechanism that guarantees the union of the fixed- and random-effects graphs remains acyclic. Computationally, we provide a provably convergent first-order method and leverage efficient batched updates across clusters. Statistically, we establish identifiability of the model and show that our approach recovers the true structure asymptotically. In experiments on real and synthetic data, our proposal detects dependencies missed by alternative estimators, underscoring its value for structure learning in clustered settings.

</details>


### [129] [An interpretable Good--Turing restart criterion for k-means++](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08243)
*Renato Cordeiro de Amorim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GTRC is a principled restart criterion for k-means++ that uses Good-Turing estimation and confidence bounds to stop restarting once further improvements become unlikely, adapting the number of restarts to dataset difficulty rather than using a fixed rule.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The number of restarts for k-means++ is typically chosen arbitrarily and applied uniformly, wasting computation on easy datasets and under-serving hard ones, undermining fair comparisons.

Method: GTRC combines a Good-Turing estimate, a proven unconditional bound, and a confidence-based bound on the probability that an additional restart improves the current result, stopping when this probability falls below a user-specified tolerance ε.

Result: Across 36 datasets, GTRC achieved clustering quality competitive with well-chosen fixed restart counts while using a variable number of restarts that appropriately reflects dataset difficulty.

Conclusion: GTRC offers a principled, interpretable, and reportable alternative to fixing the number of k-means++ restarts in advance.

Abstract: The k-means++ algorithm is commonly restarted multiple times to avoid poor local optima, yet the number of restarts is almost always chosen arbitrarily and applied uniformly regardless of data set difficulty. This undermines any comparison relying on such a choice and wastes computation on easy data sets while potentially under-serving hard ones. We introduce GTRC, a restart criterion combining a Good-Turing estimate, a proven unconditional bound, and a confidence-based bound on the probability that a further restart would improve on the current result, stopping once this probability falls below a user-specified tolerance $\varepsilon$. Across 36 data sets, GTRC reached clustering quality competitive with well-chosen fixed restart counts, while the number of restarts used varied considerably and appropriately with data set difficulty, governed by an interpretable, data-dependent signal rather than a fixed rule. GTRC offers a principled and reportable alternative to fixing the number of $k$-means++ restarts in advance. Software:https://github.com/RCdeAmorim/Good-Turing-Restart-Criterion.

</details>


### [130] [CASL-VAE: Learning Structured Latent Variables from Unpaired Data for Semi-supervised Clustering and Paired Sample Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08254)
*Sai Spandana Chintapalli,Pratik Chaudhari,Christos Davatzikos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CASL-VAE is a deep contrastive latent variable model that learns structured latent factors from unpaired data, factorizing variation into common factors and hierarchical salient factors (discrete subtypes + continuous within-subtype variation), enabling subtype recovery and paired-sample generation validated on neuroimaging and Alzheimer's disease data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantifying variability in a target population relative to a reference population (e.g., diseased vs. healthy) is central to many problems, but existing methods struggle to separate multiple modes of target-specific variation from unpaired data in the presence of heterogeneous target variation.

Method: CASL-VAE factorizes variation into continuous common latent factors (shared across populations) and hierarchical salient latent factors (discrete subtypes + continuous within-subtype variation for target-specific heterogeneity). Uses variational inference for approximate joint likelihood optimization over reference and target domains with unpaired data.

Result: Validated on semi-synthetic neuroimaging data, showing improved subtype recovery and paired-sample generation over baseline clustering and generative models. Also demonstrated ability to reveal biologically plausible heterogeneity in Alzheimer's disease.

Conclusion: CASL-VAE provides a principled framework for cross-domain analysis and paired-sample generation from unpaired data, successfully modeling heterogeneous target variation through structured latent factor decomposition.

Abstract: Quantifying variability in a target population relative to a reference population is central to many scientific and clinical problems (e.g., diseased vs. healthy). Yet, without paired data and in the presence of heterogeneous target variation, existing methods struggle to separate multiple modes of target-specific variation. We propose \textit{CASL-VAE}, a deep contrastive latent variable model that learns structured latent generative factors from unpaired data. CASL-VAE factorizes variation into continuous common latent factors shared across populations and hierarchical salient latent factors that model target-specific heterogeneity as discrete subtypes and continuous within-subtype variation. Using variational inference, we show how approximate joint likelihood optimization over reference and target domains can be performed using unpaired data, providing a principled basis for paired-sample generation and cross-domain analysis. We validate CASL-VAE on semi-synthetic neuroimaging data, demonstrating improved subtype recovery and paired-sample generation compared to baseline clustering and generative models. We also validate its ability to reveal biologically plausible heterogeneity in Alzheimer's disease.

</details>


### [131] [Classifier Chain-based Pathological Test Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08299)
*Abu Rafe Md Jamil,Nayan Malakar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A pathological test recommendation system using Classifier Chain and ML algorithms (Logistic Regression best at 98.83% accuracy) with SHAP-based explainability to speed up test selection before physician consultation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Delayed diagnostic test recommendations and subjective physician interpretations hinder effective patient care; a system is needed to accelerate test selection using patient symptoms prior to consultation.

Method: Multi-label classification framed using Classifier Chain (CC) to model test dependencies; trained on custom dataset from SOUTHERN.IML pathology with expert input; compared Logistic Regression, Decision Tree, Random Forest, and Majority Voting; interpreted via SHAP explainability.

Result: Logistic Regression with CC achieved 98.83% overall accuracy; Majority Voting ensemble achieved best balance (precision 0.93, recall 0.85, F1-score 0.89). SHAP explanations aligned with established medical knowledge.

Conclusion: Classifier Chain improves efficiency of traditional algorithms for diagnostic test recommendation; the model's reasoning aligns with medical knowledge, supporting its reliability for aiding physician decision-making.

Abstract: Accurate and timely diagnoses are essential for quality patient care. However, delayed recommendation of diagnostic tests and physicians' subjective interpretations can hinder effective care. This study introduces a pathological test recommendation system that speeds up the test selection process using patient symptoms before physician consultation. The recommendation task is framed as a multi-label classification problem utilising the Classifier Chain (CC) technique to consider dependencies between tests. We collected data from the SOUTHERN.IML pathology and then created a custom dataset with the help of the expertise. Multiple machine learning algorithms, including Logistic Regression, Decision Tree, and Random Forest, were applied to compare models and identify the best fit for our study context. The Logistic Regression with CC model had the highest overall accuracy at 98.83%, while the Majority Voting ensemble model provided the best balance with a precision of 0.93, recall of 0.85, and F1-score of 0.89. To ensure transparency of the models and clinical interpretability, we used Explainable AI (XAI) techniques utilising SHAP (SHapley Additive Explanations), which identifies how each symptom is contributing to a test recommendation. The diagnostic reasoning revealed by the model was consistent with established medical knowledge of symptoms for the recommended tests, which further adds confidence to the model's reliability for diagnostic purposes. The reasoning could help physicians make logical decisions in critical scenarios. Overall, our findings suggest that CC can improve the efficiency of the traditional algorithms in diagnostic process providing accurate test recommendations.

</details>


### [132] [Learning $\mathsf{AC}^0$ under Locally Sampleable Graphical Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08303)
*Weiming Feng,Xiongxin Yang,Yixiao Yu,Yiyao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a quasipolynomial-time learner for AC^0 under graphical models with efficient local samplers, removing the polynomial-growth requirement from prior work, using a new low-degree approximation via Glauber dynamics truncation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
