</details>


### [158] [Resample or Reroute? Budget-Aware Test-Time Model Selection for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08665)
*Teng-Ruei Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a budget-aware test-time model selection policy (Resample-or-Route, RoR) that optimally allocates a per-query budget between resampling the current LLM and rerouting to a different LLM, maximizing expected correctness under an imperfect verifier. Experiments on multi-model pools show RoR achieves a better cost-quality Pareto front than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed that test-time resampling can recover selection headroom not captured by single-commit routers, but this guarantee assumes an idealized oracle with correctness labels and unconstrained budget—neither of which exists in deployment. No prior work treats resampling and rerouting as competing uses of a single per-query cost budget.

Method: Formulates budget-aware test-time model selection as a constrained optimization: given a per-query budget and an imperfect verifier, allocate budget units between resampling (staying with the committed model) and rerouting (switching to another model). Proposes an online RoR allocation policy driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost, grounded in the asymmetry between selection and sampling recoverability.

Result: Replay experiments on multi-draw correctness tensors from 11 open-weight LLMs across 4 benchmarks show RoR attains a favorable cost-quality Pareto front vs. single-route, one-commit-router, budget-aware best-of-K, cascade, and random-allocation baselines. Largest gains on the most heterogeneous benchmark. Ablation shows gains are verifier-gated (shrink with degraded verifier quality); robustness replays under real provider pricing and a label-free agreement verifier delineate boundary conditions.

Conclusion: The RoR policy effectively trades off resampling and rerouting under budget constraints, outperforming multiple baselines. The benefits depend on verifier quality and are most pronounced on heterogeneous model pools.

Abstract: Routing among large language models (LLMs) trades response quality against serving cost, motivated by the reported gap between deployed routers and a per-instance oracle. Recent analysis shows that test-time resampling can recover per-instance selection headroom that no single-commit router captures; however, that guarantee holds only under an idealized oracle equipped with correctness labels and an unconstrained budget, neither of which a deployed system has. To the best of our knowledge, no previous work treats resampling the committed model and rerouting to an alternative model as competing uses of a single per-query cost budget. Therefore, this work formulates budget-aware test-time model selection: given a per-query budget and an imperfect verifier, allocate each unit of budget between resampling and rerouting so that expected correctness is maximized. An online resample-or-reroute (RoR) allocation policy driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost is proposed, and its behavior is grounded in the recoverability asymmetry between selection and sampling. Replay experiments on newly regenerated multi-draw correctness tensors from an eleven-model open-weight pool over four benchmarks of differing difficulty show that the proposed RoR policy attains a favorable cost-quality Pareto front relative to single-route, one-commit-router, budget-aware best-of-K, cascade, and random-allocation baselines for the tested pools, with the largest gains on the most heterogeneous benchmark; an ablation further shows the gains are verifier-gated, shrinking as verifier quality degrades, and robustness replays under a provider price vector and a label-free agreement verifier delineate where the conclusions carry over.

</details>


### [159] [A Practical Investigation of Training-free Relaxed Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08690)
*Guoxuan Xia,Luka Ribar,Paul Balanca*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates training-free relaxed speculative decoding techniques that trade lossless guarantees for speed or capability gains, benchmarks them, and finds that relaxation requires careful capability evaluation and many methods need a good language model drafter rather than lightweight drafters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent work suggests that relaxing the lossless guarantee of standard speculative decoding can yield further speed-ups, capability-speed trade-offs, or even capability gains, motivating a systematic investigation of these relaxed techniques.

Method: The authors practically investigate training-free relaxed speculative decoding approaches, unify existing methods within a shared framework, benchmark them on contemporary settings, and distill empirical findings for practitioners.

Result: Relaxation can require considerable capability evaluation unlike lossless speculative decoding; many relaxed approaches rely on a drafter that is a good language model, making them unsuited for lightweight dedicated multi-token-prediction drafters.

Conclusion: Practitioners should be aware that relaxed speculative decoding methods may not work well with lightweight drafters and require careful capability assessment.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates sampling from an autoregressive LLM by using a faster auxiliary model to draft tokens which are then verified in parallel by the LLM. Standard speculative decoding is lossless: its rejection and resampling steps exactly preserve the LLM's sampling distribution. Recent work argues that relaxing this strict guarantee can yield further speed-ups, controlled capability-speed trade-offs, or even capability gains. We practically investigate training-free relaxed speculative decoding techniques, unify existing approaches within a shared framework, benchmark them on contemporary settings, and distil takeaways and empirical findings for practitioners. Important takeaways include: relaxation can require considerable capability evaluation unlike lossless speculative decoding, and many relaxed approaches rely on a drafter that is a good language model, making them unsuited for lightweight dedicated multi-token-prediction drafters.

</details>


### [160] [MPFlow: Learning Budgeted Max-Flow Optimization on the Lightning Network with Deep Graph Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08703)
*Harrison Rush,Vincent Davis,Simone Antonelli,Vikash Singh,Jesse Shrader,Emanuele Rossi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a graph reinforcement learning approach for optimal liquidity placement in the Bitcoin Lightning Network, formulating it as a budget-constrained max-flow maximization problem solved via a message-passing PPO agent with hub-exclusion curriculum training, outperforming heuristics and deployed in production for 4640 channel decisions totaling 267.3 BTC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the problem of optimal channel selection for liquidity placement in the Lightning Network under a fixed budget, where nodes need to decide which channels to open to maximize their routing capacity (measured by s-t max-flow).

Method: Formulated as a budget-constrained combinatorial optimization problem selecting k edge additions to maximize s-t max-flow. Solved with a graph reinforcement learning agent combining a message-passing policy network with PPO and action masking, trained under a hub-exclusion curriculum that removes top hubs from training subgraphs to force capacity-aware learning.

Result: Outperforms strong heuristic baselines on the max-flow objective across multiple seeds and unseen graphs in experiments on real Lightning Network snapshots. Deployed in production, executing 4640 channel-open decisions allocating 267.3 BTC (~$16M) across 30 managed nodes.

Conclusion: The proposed graph RL approach provides a theory-grounded, practically deployable solution for liquidity placement in the Lightning Network, demonstrating superior performance over heuristics and real-world effectiveness.

Abstract: We address liquidity placement in the Bitcoin Lightning Network (LN): given a fixed budget, which channels should a node open to maximize its routing capacity? We cast this as a budget-constrained combinatorial optimization problem on graphs, selecting $k$ edge additions that maximize $s$--$t$ max-flow, a theory-grounded measure of routing capacity, and solve it with graph reinforcement learning. Our lightweight agent combines a message-passing policy network with proximal policy optimization (PPO) and action masking, and is trained under a hub-exclusion curriculum: the network's top hubs are removed from training subgraphs, forcing the policy to learn capacity-aware placement rather than hub attachment. In extensive experiments on real Lightning Network snapshots, our method consistently outperforms strong heuristic baselines on the max-flow objective across multiple seeds and unseen graphs. The agent has been deployed in production for peer recommendations, executing 4640 channel-open decisions that cumulatively allocate 267.3 BTC over $16 million across 30 managed nodes.

</details>


### [161] [Deep Learning for Joint Narrowband Interference Cancellation and Soft Demodulation in OFDM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08717)
*Emmanouil Kavvousanos,Francky Catthoor,Vassilis Paliouras*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified deep learning framework for narrowband interference mitigation in OFDM systems, combining NBI-CNet (physics-informed convolutional network for interference estimation/cancellation) and LLR-CNet (neural network for robust soft demodulation from non-Gaussian residuals). The framework eliminates error floors, achieves up to 60% complexity reduction over EOMP-IDS, delivers >3dB coding gain under mild interference, and generalizes across FFT sizes without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NBI severely degrades OFDM systems by corrupting subcarriers and rendering classical soft demodulation ineffective. Conventional CS-based mitigation suffers from high sequential latency and leaves structured non-Gaussian residuals, causing LLR unreliability, decoder saturation, and severe error floors when using classical Gaussian demappers.

Method: (1) NBI-CNet: physics-informed convolutional architecture that estimates NBI parameters and removes multi-tone interference in a single forward pass without requiring prior knowledge of active interferer count. (2) LLR-CNet: structural whitener that maps non-Gaussian post-mitigation residuals onto well-calibrated soft metrics. Together they form a unified deep learning framework for joint NBI cancellation and robust soft demodulation.

Result: Up to 60% complexity reduction (N=2048, Q=64) vs. EOMP-IDS; operates within 0.2-0.5 dB SNR margin of optimal iterative baseline at BLER=10^-4 under severe interference (SIR=-10 dB); >3 dB coding gain under mild interference (SIR=10 dB, Q=12); eliminates 2×10^-4 error floor; scale-invariant design generalizes across arbitrary FFT sizes without retraining.

Conclusion: The proposed joint deep learning framework resolves the pipeline mismatch between CS-based NBI cancellation and classical Gaussian demappers, eliminating error floors and achieving strong performance across diverse interference conditions while being computationally efficient and scale-invariant.

Abstract: Narrowband interference (NBI) severely degrades orthogonal frequency-division multiplexing (OFDM) systems by corrupting subcarriers and rendering classical soft demodulation ineffective. Conventional compressed-sensing (CS) mitigation exhibits high sequential latency and leaves structured, non-Gaussian residuals that cause log-likelihood ratio (LLR) unreliability, decoder saturation, and severe error floors when employing classical Gaussian demappers. We resolve this pipeline mismatch using a unified deep learning framework for joint NBI cancellation and robust soft demodulation. First, NBI-CNet employs a physics-informed convolutional architecture to estimate NBI parameters and remove multi-tone interference in a single forward pass. Without requiring prior knowledge of the active interferer count, NBI-CNet reduces computational complexity by up to 60% ($N{=}2048, Q{=}64$) compared to the state-of-the-art EOMP-IDS algorithm. Second, LLR-CNet acts as a structural whitener by mapping non-Gaussian post-mitigation residuals onto well-calibrated soft metrics. Simulations demonstrate that this joint framework eliminates the error floors inherent to traditional baselines across dense grids. Under severe interference ($\text{SIR}{=}{-}10$ dB), the pipeline operates within a $0.2$ to $0.5$ dB SNR margin of the optimal iterative baseline at a target block error rate (BLER) of $10^{-4}$. Under mild interference ($\text{SIR}{=}10$ dB) with heavy spectral overlap ($Q{=}12$), where classical greedy algorithms erroneously subtract valid data components and corrupt the payload, NBI-CNet avoids signal-peak confusion to deliver a coding gain exceeding $3$ dB. Finally, the architecture circumvents the $2{\times}10^{-4}$ error floor triggered by interferer-estimation errors, while its scale-invariant design enables robust generalization across arbitrary FFT sizes without retraining.

</details>


### [162] [Latent Memory Palace: Reasoning for Control as Autoregressive Variational Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08724)
*Chuning Zhu,Eva Xu,Jose Barreiros,Krishnan Srinivasan,Paarth Shah,Abhishek Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LMP introduces latent-space reasoning for control policies via variational inference, enabling adaptive test-time compute allocation and achieving strong results in simulation and real-world tasks, along with a variable-length action tokenizer that improves autoregressive policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humans exhibit flexible decision-making with adaptive deliberation times. While language models show similar reasoning capabilities, transferring this to continuous control policies is difficult because reasoning in language space lacks spatial granularity for precise motions. The authors aim to enable adaptive reasoning for control policies without relying on language.

Method: LMP formulates reasoning as variational inference with an autoregressive latent distribution, deriving a latent-space reinforcement learning technique to optimize its variational lower bound. The approach organizes information in an autoregressive latent space (memory palace) where retrieval is iterative and adaptive.

Result: LMP-π achieves strong empirical performance in simulation and real-world domains with interpretable, adaptive test-time compute allocation. LMP-tok, a variable-length action tokenizer derived from the same framework, significantly improves downstream autoregressive policies.

Conclusion: Latent reasoning for control via variational inference offers a new perspective, enabling adaptive compute allocation and strong performance without language-based reasoning, with benefits extending to action tokenization for autoregressive models.

Abstract: Human decision-making is highly flexible -- some actions are taken immediately; others require longer deliberation. Language models have exhibited a similar capacity for adaptive "reasoning." However, transferring this capability to continuous control policies has been challenging, as directly reasoning in language space may lack the granularity for spatial understanding and precise motions. In this work, we show that reasoning for control policies can emerge by organizing information in an autoregressive latent space reminiscent of a memory palace, where retrieval is iterative and adaptive. Our method, Latent Memory Palace (LMP), formulates reasoning as variational inference with an autoregressive latent distribution. We derive a latent-space reinforcement learning technique to tractably optimize its variational lower bound. The resulting policy, LMP-$π$, achieves strong empirical performance in simulation and real-world domains while exhibiting interpretable, adaptive allocation of test-time compute. We further show that the same framework yields a variable-length action tokenizer, LMP-$\texttt{tok}$, which significantly improves the performance of downstream autoregressive policies. Together, these results present a new perspective on latent reasoning for control through the lens of variational inference.

</details>


### [163] [Super Weights in LLMs and the Failure of Selective Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08733)
*Shreyas Subramanian,Adewale Akinfaderin,Akarsha Sehwag*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Super Weights (critical individual parameters) are not universally important across LLMs, cannot be effectively fine-tuned in isolation (collapsing to random performance), and require structured low-rank updates across entire layers like LoRA to succeed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether Super Weights—individual parameters that cause severe degradation when pruned—are actually trainable in isolation, and to test the hypothesis that parameter importance implies trainability.

Method: Super Weight-aware training (isolating 100–8,192 Super Weight parameters and their local neighborhoods up to 36K parameters) on OLMo-1B and OLMo-7B; comparison with random parameter training in the same layers; ablation using LoRA constrained to Super Weight coordinates across 10 seeds.

Result: Training Super Weights in isolation drops accuracy to random-guessing; expanding to local neighborhoods provides no improvement. Random parameters in the same layers improve over baseline. Vanilla LoRA (0.16% parameters) succeeds, but constraining LoRA updates to Super Weight coordinates yields statistically indistinguishable results from the failure case.

Conclusion: Parameter importance (via pruning) does not imply parameter trainability in isolation. Effective fine-tuning requires structured decompositions over entire layers rather than targeting individually important weights.

Abstract: Recent work identified Super Weights, individual parameters whose removal degrades model performance by orders of magnitude. We show that this degradation due to pruning Super Weights does not universally apply to all LLMs. Furthermore, if these parameters are so important, Super Weight-aware training should be effective. We show the opposite. Training Super Weights in isolation (100 to 8,192 parameters) drops accuracy to random-guessing levels on both OLMo-1B and OLMo-7B, and expanding to local neighborhoods of up to 36K parameters provides no improvement. The failure is specific to Super Weight coordinates: training an equal number of randomly chosen positions in the same down_proj layers instead improves over the baseline, so the collapse comes from targeting Super Weights, not from sparsity itself. Vanilla LoRA, updating every position in attention weight matrices through low-rank structure, succeeds with only 0.16% of parameters, and applying the same low-rank update to down_proj succeeds as well. A 10-seed ablation confirms that constraining LoRA updates at positions corresponding to Super Weight coordinates yields statistically indistinguishable results. These findings establish that parameter importance does not imply parameter trainability in isolation, and that effective fine-tuning relies on structured decompositions over entire layers rather than targeting individually important weights.

</details>


### [164] [Dimensionality Reduction Meets Network Science: Sensemaking on UMAP's kNN Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08746)
*Duen Horng Chau,Donghao Ren,Fred Hohman,Dominik Moritz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that the internal k-nearest-neighbor graph UMAP constructs can be leveraged for data sensemaking using standard graph algorithms (PageRank, k-core decomposition, clustering coefficient) to identify representative points, dense regions, and tight-knit neighborhoods, competing with or complementing purpose-built methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Typical UMAP workflows focus on the lower-dimensional embedding, overlooking the rich kNN graph constructed internally, which encodes the data manifold before distortion from 2D projection.

Method: Apply standard graph algorithms (PageRank for representative point identification, k-core decomposition for dense vs. peripheral regions, clustering coefficient for tight-knit neighborhoods) to UMAP's internal kNN graph.

Result: Quantitative and qualitative evaluation on MNIST and Fashion MNIST shows these graph-based analyses are practical and competitive with or complementary to purpose-built methods (e.g., k-medoids, HDBSCAN).

Conclusion: UMAP's internal kNN graph has untapped potential for enhanced data sensemaking via standard graph algorithms.

Abstract: While UMAP is widely used for exploring high-dimensional data, typical workflows focus on its lower-dimensional embedding, largely overlooking the rich k-nearest-neighbor (kNN) graph that UMAP constructs internally. This graph encodes the data manifold in its original high-dimensional space, before the distortion that UMAP's 2D projection introduces. We demonstrate the untapped potential of this internal representation, showing how standard graph algorithms applied to this graph enhance data sensemaking: (1) PageRank identifies representative data points, (2) k-core decomposition reveals dense core regions versus sparse periphery, and (3) clustering coefficient detects tight-knit neighborhoods with highly-similar data points. Through quantitative and qualitative evaluation on MNIST and Fashion MNIST, we show that these graph-based analyses are not only practical but also competitive with or complementary to purpose-built methods (e.g., k-medoids for exemplar selection, HDBSCAN for density-based clustering).

</details>


### [165] [SLORR: Simple and Efficient In-Training Low-Rank Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08754)
*David González-Martínez,Shiwei Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SLORR is a simple, stateless, architecture-preserving framework for in-training low-rank regularization that uses GPU-friendly approximations of Hoyer sparsity and nuclear norm metrics to induce compressibility in neural networks with minimal training overhead and without requiring SVD or architectural modifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing low-rank regularization methods for neural network compression suffer from limitations: they often require expensive SVD computations on large weight matrices, modify model architecture by introducing additional trainable parameters, or rely on stateful cached quantities. The authors aim to address these shortcomings with a simpler, more efficient approach.

Method: SLORR directly regularizes original weight matrices using GPU-friendly approximations for forward and backward passes of two variants: one based on the Hoyer sparsity metric and another based on the nuclear norm. The framework is stateless (no cached quantities) and architecture-preserving (no added parameters). The authors provide approximation guarantees for their GPU-friendly formulations.

Result: On ImageNet-1K, SLORR induces compressibility in ResNet-50, ViT-B/16, and ViT-L/16 (continued training) and ResNet-18 (pretraining) with less than 8% training overhead. For LLM pretraining at 135M and 560M scales, SLORR-Hoyer preserves model performance substantially better than unregularized baselines when compressed, while adding less than 1% average training overhead.

Conclusion: SLORR provides an efficient and practical solution for in-training low-rank regularization that avoids the computational and architectural drawbacks of prior methods, making it suitable for both vision models and large language models at scale.

Abstract: Low-rank factorization is widely used to compress neural networks, but modern models are often not naturally amenable to aggressive factorization without significant accuracy loss. Existing training-time low-rank regularizers can improve compressibility, but they often require SVDs of large weight matrices, modify the model architecture (introducing additional trainable parameters), or rely on stateful cached quantities. To address these limitations, we introduce SLORR, a simple, stateless, and architecture-preserving framework for in-training low-rank regularization, instantiated with two main variants based on the Hoyer sparsity metric and the nuclear norm. SLORR directly regularizes the original weight matrices using GPU-friendly approximations for the forward and backward passes of the regularizers, for which we provide approximation guarantees. We first evaluate SLORR on ImageNet-1K across short-horizon continued training of ResNet-50, ViT-B/16, and ViT-L/16, and pretraining of ResNet-18, where SLORR induces compressibility while introducing less than 8% training overhead. We further evaluate SLORR-Hoyer in LLM pretraining at 135M and 560M scales: SLORR-trained compressed models preserve performance substantially better than unregularized models while adding less than 1% average training overhead.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [166] [REFORGE: A Method for Benchmarking LLMs' Reverse Engineering Capabilities in Decompiled Binary Function Naming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07738)
*Nicolas Koller,Andreas u. Schmidt*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Reforge is a provenance-tracked pipeline for constructing reliable function-level ground truth in LLM-assisted binary analysis. It reveals that binary-to-source alignment uncertainty under compiler optimization is a major obstacle to fair evaluation, not model capability. High-confidence yield drops from 87.2% to 65.9% across optimization levels, and unpaired comparisons introduce survivorship bias. A proof-of-concept evaluation on seven LLMs for function naming demonstrates the need for uncertainty-aware benchmarking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for LLM-assisted binary analysis treat function-level ground truth construction as a solved problem and fail to account for alignment uncertainty caused by compiler optimization, leading to overestimated and unreliable capability claims.

Method: Reforge pipeline: constructs function-level ground truth from C source through compilation, DWARF and syntactic extraction, alignment, and decompilation; operationalizes alignment uncertainty via an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification.

Result: High-confidence yield drops from 87.2% to 65.9% across optimization levels; unpaired comparisons overstate optimization-induced performance decay through survivorship bias.

Conclusion: Fair evaluation of LLMs on binary analysis requires uncertainty-aware benchmarking that accounts for alignment reliability under compiler optimization, rather than treating ground truth as uniformly reliable.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to reverse-engineering tasks, and recent threat-intelligence reporting shows them operating inside live offensive-security workflows. Claims about their capability, however, outpace our ability to measure it. Existing benchmarks for LLM-assisted binary analysis treat the construction of function-level ground truth as a solved pre-processing step and report accuracy without disclosing how many functions were reliably evaluable. We argue that the principal obstacle to fair evaluation is not model capability but the reliability of binary-to-source alignment under compiler optimization. This paper presents Reforge, a provenance-tracked pipeline that constructs function-level ground truth from C source through compilation, DWARF and syntactic extraction, alignment, and decompilation, and that operationalizes alignment uncertainty as an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification. On a controlled micro-benchmark, high-confidence yield falls from 87.2% to 65.9% across optimization levels, and unpaired comparisons overstate optimization-induced performance decay through survivorship bias. A proof-of-concept evaluation of seven contemporary LLMs on function naming demonstrates the substrate and motivates uncertainty-aware benchmarking practice

</details>


### [167] [PERFOPT-Bench: Evaluating Coding Agents on Software Performance Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07744)
*Yingyun Cui,Yi Xie,Piaohong Wang,Jiawei Ma,Bo Liu,Liangliang Cao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: PERFOPT-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating the full performance-engineering loop of code agents, including profiling, diagnosing bottlenecks, editing code, and verifying speedups. Evaluation of 7 agent stacks on 7 tasks shows performance is workload-dependent, raw speedup is misleading due to shortcut exploitation, and agent framework choice significantly impacts same-LLM performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding-agent benchmarks focus on functional correctness (patch generation) but ignore performance optimization — a distinct task requiring profiling, cross-layer diagnosis, correctness-preserving edits, and reproducibility verification. Production software demands measurable speedups, so a dedicated benchmark for the full optimization loop is needed.

Method: Introduced PERFOPT-Bench, where each task provides a correct but suboptimal codebase with a target metric to improve. Scoring combines hidden correctness tests, verified-speedup measurement, and trajectory-level audit. Evaluated 7 agent stacks (varying LLMs and frameworks) across 7 long-horizon optimization tasks.

Result: No single agent stack dominates; performance is workload-dependent. Changing the agent framework materially changes the same LLM's per-task speedup profile. Raw speedup is unsafe as a score because some large gains come from benchmark-specific shortcut exploitation. A relay pilot shows restarting from an externalized optimization summary can recover additional headroom.

Conclusion: Performance optimization is a distinct, under-benchmarked agentic task requiring holistic evaluation beyond functional correctness. Benchmark design must guard against shortcut exploitation, and multi-stage or relay approaches may improve optimization outcomes.

Abstract: Coding-agent benchmarks have largely measured whether agents can produce functionally correct patches, but production software also demands measurable speedups on real execution targets. Performance optimization is a distinct agentic task: agents must profile executions, diagnose cross-layer bottlenecks, edit code without breaking correctness, and verify that gains are reproducible rather than measurement artifacts. We introduce PERFOPT-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this full performance-engineering loop. Each task provides a correct but deliberately suboptimal codebase and asks the agent to improve a target performance metric; scoring requires hidden correctness tests, verified-speedup measurement, and trajectory-level audit. We evaluate 7 agent stacks with different LLMs and agent frameworks on 7 long-horizon optimization tasks. The results show that optimization performance is workload-dependent rather than determined by model identity alone: no single stack dominates, and changing the agent framework can materially change the same LLM's per-task speedup profile. We further find that raw speedup is unsafe as a benchmark score, since some large gains arise from benchmark-specific shortcut exploitation; an exploratory relay pilot suggests that restarting from an externalized optimization summary can recover additional headroom after an initial session stops. The benchmark and our evaluation are available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Dataset-D3CC.

</details>


### [168] [Functional and Secure Code Generation with Task Vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07881)
*Felix Wang,Anudeep Das,Mei Nagappan,N. Asokan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SecVecCoder uses task-vector arithmetic on LLM weights to generate code that is both functional and secure, improving trustworthy code completions by 2.1-36.0 percentage points across six coding LLMs without post-generation fixes or significant latency overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle to generate code that is both functional and free of security vulnerabilities; prior work evaluates functionality and security separately or only fixes issues post-generation, lacking an integrated alignment approach for secure code generation.

Method: SecVecCoder leverages task-vector arithmetic (linear operations on LLM weights) to simultaneously enhance both functionality and security alignment of coding LLMs, requiring no method-specific decoding or post-generation adjustments.

Result: Across six coding LLMs from three families on CodeGuard+, SecVecCoder improves trustworthy code completions by 2.1-36.0 percentage points over the base model, with improvements on unseen CWE types reaching up to 39.1 percentage points, while adding only 0.6% latency overhead on average.

Conclusion: Task-vector arithmetic is effective for jointly aligning code LLMs toward functional and secure outputs, achieving substantial improvements at minimal computational cost without post-processing.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for code generation, but they struggle to generate functional code free of security vulnerabilities. Prior work to improve the secure code generation abilities of such coding LLMs has largely focused on evaluating code functionality and security separately using different datasets, or focused on finding vulnerabilities post-generation. At the same time, the text-generation domain has seen significant work on alignment techniques, where models are tuned such that their outputs exhibit certain qualities (e.g., helpfulness, harmlessness). Of particular interest is task-vector arithmetic, where linear operations on LLM weights can be used to arbitrarily enhance alignment while incurring only minimal computational overhead. We develop a novel method, SecVecCoder, leveraging task vectors to produce trustworthy code that is simultaneously functional and secure without the need for post-generation adjustment. Across six coding LLMs from three families on the CodeGuard+ benchmark, SecVecCoder improves the rate of trustworthy code completions by 2.1-36.0 percentage points over the base model, with improvements on unseen CWE types reaching up to 39.1 percentage points. Since the effectiveness of the coding LLM relies only on changing the model weights, SecVecCoder requires no method-specific decoding and hence achieves a decoding latency within 0.6% of the base model's, on average.

</details>


### [169] [Bug Report Specification Refinement with Trajectory Guidance for Automated Program Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07882)
*S M Farah Al Fahim,Md Nakhla Rafi,Md Ahasanuzzaman,Zeyang Ma,Dong Jae Kim,Shaowei Wang,Tse-Hsun,Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TrajSpec refines bug reports by running a trajectory-collection agent on the pre-fix repository, extracting hierarchical evidence (interpretation, diagnosis, observations), and applying repository-based review, improving Pass@1 across multiple APR agents (e.g., from 41% to 59.67% with GPT-5-mini on SWE-Bench Lite).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bug reports used as task specifications for repository-level APR agents often lack key information (failure-inducing behavior, behavioral requirement, implementation scope), causing agents to inspect irrelevant code, infer incorrect requirements, or produce symptom-level patches that fail to restore intended behavior.

Method: TrajSpec operates in two phases: (1) Run a trajectory-collection agent on the pre-fix repository to obtain an unverified trajectory, then organize evidence into a three-level hierarchy (high-level interpretation, diagnostic findings, concrete observations). (2) Generate a draft refined report and apply repository-based review to remove unsupported claims, revise uncertain claims, and add repository-supported details.

Result: On all 300 SWE-Bench Lite instances, TrajSpec improves Pass@1 from 41.00% to 59.67% (GPT-5-mini) and from 54.67% to 64.33% (MiniMax M2.5). On a stratified sample of 100 instances, Pass@1 improves from 41.00% to 71.00% (Agentless) and from 47.00% to 72.00% (AutoCodeRover). Ablations show dropping repository-based review or hierarchical evidence reduces Pass@1 from 59.67% to 48.00% and 47.67%.

Conclusion: TrajSpec provides actionable repository-supported context that consistently improves repair performance across multiple APR agents, demonstrating the value of trajectory-derived evidence and structured refinement for bug report specification.

Abstract: Bug reports serve as task specifications for repository-level automated program repair (APR) agents, but they often describe only the observed failure and omit repair-relevant information such as the failure-inducing behavior, behavioral requirement, and implementation scope. As a result, a repair agent may inspect irrelevant code, infer an incorrect requirement, or generate a patch that addresses the reported symptom without restoring the intended repository behavior. We present TrajSpec, a trajectory-guided approach for repository-supported bug report specification refinement. Given an original report and a pre-fix repository, TrajSpec runs a trajectory-collection agent and uses the resulting unverified trajectory as a source of trajectory-derived specification evidence. It organizes this evidence into a three-level representation consisting of a high-level interpretation of the issue, diagnostic findings supporting that interpretation, and concrete repository observations. TrajSpec then generates a draft refined report and applies repository-based review to remove unsupported claims, revise uncertain claims, and add repository-supported details. We evaluate TrajSpec on all 300 SWE-Bench Lite instances using Mini-SWE-Agent V2. TrajSpec's refined reports improve Pass@1 from 41.00% to 59.67% with GPT-5-mini and from 54.67% to 64.33% with MiniMax M2.5. On a stratified sample of 100 instances, TrajSpec's refined reports also improve Pass@1 from 41.00% to 71.00% with Agentless and from 47.00% to 72.00% with AutoCodeRover. Ablation results show that removing repository-based review or the hierarchical evidence representation reduces Pass@1 from 59.67% to 48.00% and 47.67%, respectively. Overall, TrajSpec provides actionable repository-supported context that consistently improves repair performance.

</details>


### [170] [The Memory Wall of Green Software: Empirical Energy Evaluation of Memento Design Pattern](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07944)
*Imane Jriri,Tarik Houichime,Younes El Amrani*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper empirically investigates the energy consumption of the Memento design pattern, comparing Classic (full-snapshot) and Differential (delta-encoding) strategies against a direct baseline. Using RAPL hardware telemetry across 10–200 MB state volumes, they find Differential reduces energy up to 65.8% for mid-scale states but collapses at 200 MB due to GC thrashing and power spikes, yielding evidence-based heuristics for energy-aware software design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As energy efficiency becomes a critical non-functional requirement in Green Software Engineering, the "metabolic cost" of abstraction layers in software design patterns remains poorly understood. The paper aims to expose and quantify this hidden energy overhead, specifically for the Memento pattern.

Method: Empirical benchmarking using the RAPL (Running Average Power Limit) hardware interface for precise energy telemetry. Three implementations were compared: a direct unabstracted baseline, Classic Memento (full-snapshot), and Differential Memento (delta-encoding), tested across state sizes from 10 MB to 200 MB.

Result: The Differential strategy achieves up to 65.8% energy reduction for mid-scale states due to minimized memory traffic. However, at 200 MB, it hits a "memory wall" where GC thrashing and non-linear power spikes completely neutralize algorithmic optimizations, with Classic also severely degraded.

Conclusion: Structural design pattern choices have significant, non-linear energy implications. Evidence-based heuristics are synthesized to help architects reconcile design quality with Green IT imperatives, emphasizing that delta-encoding gains are bounded by memory saturation effects.

Abstract: As Green Software Engineering matures, energy efficiency has transitioned into a mission-critical non-functional requirement. While software design patterns ensure structural integrity, their inherent abstraction layers impose an implicit "metabolic cost" that often remains obscured during the design phase. This paper empirically investigates the energy dynamics of the Memento design pattern, contrasting a direct, unabstracted baseline against Classic full-snapshot and Differential delta-encoding strategies. Leveraging the RAPL interface for high-fidelity hardware telemetry, we quantify energy dissipation across state volumes scaling from 10 MB to 200 MB. Our empirical results expose a critical architectural trade-off: the Differential strategy minimizes memory traffic, yielding a maximum energy reduction of 65.8% for mid-scale states, but collides with a catastrophic "memory wall" at 200 MB. At this saturation point, algorithmic optimizations are completely neutralized by severe GC thrashing and non-linear power spikes. We synthesize these findings into evidence-based heuristics, providing architects with a robust framework to reconcile structural design quality with sustainable Green IT imperatives.

</details>


### [171] [DeepSWE: Measuring Frontier Coding Agents on Original, Long-Horizon Engineering Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07946)
*Wenqi Huang,Charley Lee,Leonard Tng,Serena Ge*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DeepSWE is a new benchmark of 113 original, long-horizon software engineering tasks designed to address data contamination and evaluation flaws in existing coding benchmarks like SWE-bench, using hand-written verifiers and tasks that stay out of public training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding benchmarks like SWE-bench suffer from data contamination (fixes/tasks may appear in pretraining data) and flawed evaluation (tests written for specific fixes can fail correct alternatives or pass incomplete solutions).

Method: DeepSWE creates 113 original tasks across 91 repositories and 5 languages, never contributed back upstream; each task uses a hand-written verifier that checks requested functionality rather than specific test cases, and an LLM judge re-reviews graded runs for validation.

Result: The LLM judge disagrees with DeepSWE's verifier only 1.4% of the time vs 32.4% for SWE-Bench Pro's inherited tests. DeepSWE prompts are half the length but reference solutions touching 5.5x more code, and it better separates frontier agent performance across a wider score band.

Conclusion: DeepSWE provides a more contamination-resistant and evaluation-faithful benchmark for coding agents, with better discriminative power between state-of-the-art systems.

Abstract: DeepSWE is a benchmark of 113 original, long-horizon software engineering tasks for evaluating coding agents. Most public agentic coding benchmarks follow SWE-bench in mining merged fixes from public GitHub repositories, which creates two problems: the fixes and their discussion were likely seen during pretraining, so a high score can reflect recall rather than problem-solving; and each task is graded by the tests that shipped with its merged fix, which were written to confirm one specific fix rather than grade an arbitrary solution, so they can fail a correct alternative or pass an incomplete one. DeepSWE avoids both. Its tasks are written from scratch across 91 active open-source repositories and five languages and are never contributed back upstream, so their reference solutions stay out of the public record that model training scrapes; and each task is graded by a hand-written verifier that checks the requested functionality and accepts any implementation that provides it. When an independent LLM judge re-reviews graded runs, it disagrees with DeepSWE's verifier about an order of magnitude less often than with SWE-Bench Pro's inherited tests (1.4% versus 32.4%). Despite being about half the length of SWE-Bench Pro's prompts, DeepSWE's prompts describe tasks whose reference solutions touch 5.5x more code, and the benchmark separates frontier agents across a wider score band than the leaderboards on which they otherwise cluster. We release the benchmark, its verifiers, and the full record of evaluation trajectories.

</details>


### [172] [3100 Opinions on Code Review in an AI World: Building Causal Theory from Practitioner Discourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07980)
*Shyam Agarwal,Courtney Miller,Christian Kästner,Bogdan Vasilescu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper examines how AI coding agents affect code review through a mixed-methods approach: first identifying unstable trends via GitHub repository mining, then building a causal theory from 38,709 grey-literature documents. The resulting model posits that code review acts as the control point where a team's expertise and process structure determine whether AI's impact is positive or negative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners sharply disagree about whether AI coding agents make code review a bottleneck, eliminate the need for human review, or erode developer understanding. Prior repository-mining studies yield unstable and contradictory trends without explaining underlying mechanisms.

Method: (1) Observational analysis of public GitHub activity comparing agent-authored vs. human-authored PRs. (2) Large-scale grey-literature synthesis: collected 38,709 documents (engineering blogs, Reddit), filtered to code-review-relevant content, coded a stratified random sample of 3,100 via LLM-assisted pipeline. (3) Built a causal model with 26 constructs and 67 relationships (64 directed, 3 contested).

Result: Agent-authored PRs are reviewed less often, merged faster, and discussed less—but these trends flip under different analysis choices. The causal theory organizes findings around review as the "control point" where a team's expertise and review-process structure determine AI's effect sign.

Conclusion: AI does not fix the sign of its effect on software quality; the team sets it through human expertise and process design. The paper provides a falsifiable theoretical framework and contributes an LLM-assisted grey-literature methodology as a reusable template for SE research.

Abstract: Coding agents now author entire pull requests, and practitioners sharply disagree about what this does to code review: whether it becomes the bottleneck, whether human review is still necessary, and whether it quietly erodes the understanding that it once built. Repository-mining studies measure surface trends but seldom explain the mechanisms beneath them, and the trends themselves prove unstable. A motivating observational analysis of public GITHUB activity finds that agent-authored pull requests are reviewed less often, merged several times faster, and discussed less than human-authored ones, yet the direction of these trends flips under different but equally defensible analysis choices, so the traces establish what is changing without explaining why. To recover the mechanisms, we synthesize practitioner discourse at scale into an explanatory theory: we collect 38,709 grey-literature documents (engineering blogs and Reddit threads), filter to those substantively about code review, and code a stratified random sample of 3,100 with an LLM-assisted pipeline, from which we build a causal model of 26 constructs and 67 relationships (64 directed, 3 contested). Its organizing claim is that review is the control point through which a coding agent's effect on software is decided, and that AI does not fix the sign of that effect: the team sets it, through the expertise its humans bring and how it structures the review process. The theory makes the competing positions explicit and turns "AI is changing code review" into falsifiable propositions with named constructs and moderators. As a secondary contribution, we offer the underlying LLM-assisted, grey-literature theory-building method as a scalable template for software-engineering research, with a public implementation.

</details>


### [173] [On the Correctness of Software Merge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07987)
*Akira Mori,Masatomo Hashimoto*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a structural merge tool based on formal correctness criteria (parsable and universal) using category theory pushouts, evaluated on 43,774 Java merge scenarios, showing fewer errors than existing tools like Git.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Three-way merge tools lack formal correctness criteria; their results are often accepted intuitively without systematic scrutiny, leading to undetected errors in practice.

Method: The authors define two syntactic criteria for merge correctness: parsable (syntactically valid per language grammar) and universal (incorporates all and only edits from each branch, with shared edits applied once). This is formalized via pushouts in category theory. A structural merge tool implementing these criteria is presented and compared against existing tools.

Result: In a large-scale experiment with 43,774 merge scenarios from 76 open-source Java projects, existing tools (e.g., Git) produced incorrect results, while the proposed tool reported none. Additional experiments with 2,582 developer-resolved merges and 2,459 refactoring scenarios revealed both strengths and limitations of structural merge.

Conclusion: Clear correctness criteria are crucial for reliable merge tools. The proposed criteria (parsable and universal) provide a foundation for developing more principled merge tools, though structural merge still has limitations that warrant further research.

Abstract: Three-way merge tools play crucial roles in modern software development, where a developer forks a branch to make local modifications and requests it to be merged into the main branch via a "pull request." Despite its importance, the task has traditionally been defined in an intuitive manner, and the results of merge tools are often accepted without scrutiny. In this paper, we present a new structural merge tool in comparison with existing tools based on the syntactic criteria we propose for evaluating the merge results. We require the merge result to be both parsable and universal. Being parsable means that the result is syntactically valid according to the grammar of the programming language. Being universal means that the result incorporates all and only the edit operations occurring in each branch while ensuring that edits common to both branches are applied only once. This requirement can be precisely defined using the notion of pushouts in category theory. In a large-scale experiment involving 43,774 file merge scenarios from 76 open-source Java projects, we found a number of incorrect results reported by existing tools such as the Git companion merge tool, whereas our tool reports none. We further compared d3j's results with 2,582 developer-resolved merges and with 2,459 merge scenarios involving 21 refactoring types. These experiments revealed both the strengths and current limitations of structural merge, and underscore the importance of clear correctness criteria. We expect that the proposed criterion will provide a foundation for developing more reliable and principled merge tools.

</details>


### [174] [Aleena: Alignment Agent for Research Software Engineering Collaborations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08043)
*Kshitij Dani,Cordero Core,Landung Setiawan,Carlos Garcia Jurado Suarez,Anshul Tambay,Vani Mandava,Anant Mittal*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Aleena is an open-source agentic AI system that tracks multi-modal stakeholder interactions on GitHub to preserve decision rationale, surface risks, and maintain alignment in research software engineering projects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research software collaborations generate decisions across disparate artifacts (Slack, meetings, pull requests, issues), causing divergence in mental models between domain researchers and research software engineers regarding project intent, ownership, and scientific assumptions.

Method: The authors developed Aleena, an open-source lifecycle alignment agent that uses GitHub as a shared collaboration surface to transform multi-modal stakeholder interactions into structured project records that surface risks, track open questions, and preserve decision continuity.

Result: The paper presents the system design, prototype, and illustrative lifecycle scenarios for Aleena, grounded in university-based research software engineering center experiences.

Conclusion: Alignment in research software engineering is a continuous lifecycle problem, and agentic AI can support stakeholder alignment and project-state tracking without replacing human decision-making.

Abstract: Research software collaborations span meetings, informal chats, pull requests, and GitHub issues. A decision surfaced in a Slack thread, refined in a meeting, and implemented in a pull request can lose its original rationale across these artifacts, leaving domain researchers and research software engineers with divergent mental models of project intent, ownership, and scientific assumptions. We argue that alignment in research software engineering is a continuous lifecycle problem, and that agentic AI can support stakeholder alignment and project-state tracking without replacing human decision-making. We present Aleena, an open-source lifecycle alignment agent that uses GitHub as a shared collaboration surface, transforming multi-modal stakeholder interactions into structured project records that surface risks, track open questions, and preserve decision continuity. Grounded in university-based research software engineering center experiences, this paper presents the motivating problem, system design, prototype, and illustrative lifecycle scenarios for Aleena.

</details>


### [175] [TTHE: Test-Time Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08124)
*Jun Nie,Yonggang Zhang,Jun Song,Qianshu Cai,Dahai Yu,Yike Guo,Xinmei Tian,Bo Han*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TTHE is a test-time adaptation method that evolves the LLM agent's executable harness (context construction, tool invocation, error recovery) during evaluation using only unlabeled execution traces, without updating model weights or requiring gold labels. It improves fixed baselines across text-to-SQL, competitive programming, software engineering, data-science coding, and agentic tool-use tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches optimize agent harnesses before deployment using training/development data, yielding fixed workflows that cannot adapt to test-time distribution shifts, novel failure modes, or unseen tool interactions.

Method: TTHE maintains a population of candidate harnesses during evaluation. An agentic proposer refines them by reasoning over execution traces (no gold labels), and a judge commits improved harnesses using execution-derived proxy signals. The solver, proposer, and judge are different roles and harnesses around the same frozen LLM—all adaptation occurs through program changes, not weight updates.

Result: TTHE consistently improves fixed ReAct-style baseline harnesses across text-to-SQL, competitive programming, software engineering, data-science coding, and agentic tool-use tasks, yielding persistent and inspectable improvements.

Conclusion: Test-time adaptation for LLM agents can be recast as evolution over executable control programs, with execution-derived proxy reliability identified as a central challenge for robust unsupervised agent improvement.

Abstract: The behavior of an LLM agent is determined not only by the underlying model, but also by its harness: the executable program that constructs context, invokes tools, verifies intermediate results, and recovers from failures. Existing approaches optimize such harnesses before deployment, searching training or development data for a fixed agent workflow that is then frozen at test time. This limits adaptation when the test distribution, failure modes, or tool interactions differ from those seen during development. We ask whether the harness can instead be optimized during evaluation itself, using only the unlabeled execution traces the agent produces on the test inputs. We introduce Test-Time Harness Evolution (TTHE), which treats the executable harness as the state of test-time adaptation. During evaluation, TTHE maintains a population of candidate harnesses and refines them through an agentic proposer that reasons over their execution traces, without gold labels or task-specific supervision; a judge then commits an improved harness from execution-derived proxy signals, and the selected program persists to govern subsequent inputs. Crucially, TTHE does not update model weights, require gold labels, or train a separate adaptation model: solver, proposers, and judge are different roles and harnesses around the same frozen LLM, so all adaptation occurs through changes to the surrounding program. Across text-to-SQL, competitive programming, software engineering, data-science coding, and agentic tool-use tasks, TTHE improves fixed ReAct-style baseline harnesses, yielding persistent, inspectable improvements rather than a pre-searched workflow or per-query retries. These results recast test-time adaptation for LLM agents as evolution over executable control programs and identify execution-derived proxy reliability as a central challenge for robust unsupervised agent improvement.

</details>


### [176] [ProjAgent: Procedural Similarity Retrieval for Repository-Level Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08691)
*QiHong Chen,Aaron Imani,Iftekhar Ahmed*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ProjAgent is a repository-level code generation system that introduces procedural similarity as a novel retrieval signal, decomposes target functions into reasoning steps, uses agentic workflow to retrieve procedurally similar functions, combines with semantic retrieval, and employs static-analysis feedback loops for iterative repair, achieving 41.14% Pass@1 on REPOCOD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrieval methods for repository-level code generation rely on lexical, structural, or semantic similarity, but fail to capture functions that share similar procedural logic when identifiers or application domains differ. This missing dimension limits retrieval quality and downstream generation performance.

Method: ProjAgent decomposes the target function into intermediate reasoning steps and uses an agentic workflow to retrieve repository functions exhibiting similar procedural behavior at each step. The procedurally retrieved context is fused with conventional semantic retrieval results. A conservative static-analysis feedback loop iteratively repairs generated code using compiler and static-analysis feedback.

Result: On REPOCOD, ProjAgent achieves 41.14% Pass@1, outperforming existing retrieval-based baselines.

Conclusion: Procedural similarity is an effective and previously unexplored retrieval dimension for repository-level code generation, and combining it with semantic retrieval and static-analysis-guided repair yields substantial improvements.

Abstract: Repository-level code generation requires implementing target functions while accounting for complex cross-file dependencies and project-specific conventions. Existing retrieval methods predominantly rely on lexical, structural, or semantic similarity, often overlooking repository functions that implement similar procedural logic despite differing in identifiers or application domains. We propose ProjAgent, a repository-level code generation system that introduces procedural similarity as an explicit retrieval signal. ProjAgent decomposes the target function into intermediate reasoning steps and employs an agentic workflow to retrieve repository functions that exhibit similar procedural behavior at each step. The retrieved procedural context is integrated with conventional semantic retrieval to construct a richer repository context for code generation. ProjAgent further incorporates a conservative static-analysis feedback loop that iteratively repairs generated code using compiler and static-analysis feedback. Evaluated on REPOCOD, ProjAgent achieves 41.14% Pass@1, outperforming existing retrieval-based baselines. These results demonstrate that procedural similarity is an effective and previously unexplored retrieval dimension for repository-level code generation.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [177] [A Self-Supervised Approach for Minimal-Annotation Hydroacoustic Data Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07733)
*Pierre-Yves Raumer,Axel Marmoret,Dorian Cazau,Anatole Gros-Martial,Richard Dreo,Maelle Torterotot,Sara Bazin,Flore Samaran,Jean-Yves Royer*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A self-supervised pipeline using Masked AutoEncoder and clustering (UMAP+HDBSCAN) is proposed to explore hydroacoustic recordings without labeled data, achieving fast manual mapping to 15 classes and performance comparable to supervised detectors on a multi-year Mayotte Island dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Passive hydroacoustic monitoring generates massive unlabeled recordings; manual annotation is costly, and rare signals lack labeled data for supervised methods.

Method: A Masked AutoEncoder (MAE) is pre-trained on reconstruction pretext tasks to extract patch-level spectrogram representations. Adjacent informative patches are aggregated into event-level embeddings, then clustered via UMAP+HDBSCAN at the dataset scale.

Result: 317 clusters manually mapped to 15 hydroacoustic classes in under one hour; classifier performance comparable to two existing detectors; recovered known seasonal patterns and identified previously unstudied signals.

Conclusion: The self-supervised exploration pipeline effectively addresses the labeling bottleneck in low-frequency hydroacoustic monitoring, enabling discovery of both known and novel patterns without large annotated datasets.

Abstract: Passive hydroacoustic monitoring often generates large volumes of continuous recordings that are only partially exploited due to the cost of manual annotation. Supervised detection methods perform well but require large labeled datasets, seldom available for rare signals or understudied environments. This work proposes a self-supervised exploration pipeline to address this limitation in low-frequency settings. A Masked AutoEncoder (MAE) is pre-trained on a reconstruction pretext task, then used to extract patch-level representations from spectrograms. Within each spectrogram, adjacent informative patches are aggregated into event-level embeddings, enabling the disentanglement of overlapping events. These embeddings are then clustered at the dataset scale using the dimension reduction algorithm UMAP and the clustering algorithm HDBSCAN to identify hydroacoustic patterns. The pipeline was applied to a multi-year hydroacoustic dataset collected near Mayotte Island, Indian Ocean, containing marine mammal vocalizations, seismo-volcanic signals, and anthropogenic noise. The 317 clusters were manually mapped to 15 hydroacoustic classes or noise in less than one hour. The method was evaluated in two ways. Quantitatively, when used as a classifier, it achieved performance comparable to two existing detectors. Qualitatively, it recovered known seasonal patterns of marine mammal acoustic activity. It also identified patterns of previously unstudied signals, thereby demonstrating its practical value.

</details>


### [178] [PS4: Proxy-Supervised Joint Training for Real Target Speaker Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08111)
*Wanyi Ning,Wei Zhou,Yingpeng Li,Yinshang Guo,Haitao Qian,Yiming Cheng*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: PS4 is a proxy-supervised training framework for target speaker extraction in real conversational mixtures, using a large-scale 71,771-sample corpus and four differentiable objectives to fine-tune a BSRNN-based TSE model, achieving 2nd overall on the REAL-T challenge leaderboard with best speaker similarity and timing F1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training TSE models for real conversational mixtures is challenging due to the lack of large-scale training corpora and clean target speech for supervision.

Method: Constructs a 71,771-sample corpus from four public datasets, and proposes a proxy-supervised joint training strategy that fine-tunes a BSRNN-based TSE model using four complementary differentiable objectives: ASR cross-entropy, speaker similarity, frame-level VAD, and perceptual audio quality.

Result: PS4 ranks 2nd overall on the REAL-T challenge leaderboard, achieving the best speaker similarity and timing F1 among all submitted systems.

Conclusion: Proxy supervision with multiple differentiable objectives effectively enables TSE model training for real conversational mixtures without requiring clean target speech.

Abstract: Training target speaker extraction (TSE) models for real conversational mixtures remains challenging because large-scale training corpora and clean target speech for supervision are unavailable. We present PS4, a proxy-supervised training framework for TSE in real conversational mixtures, with two main contributions. First, we construct a large-scale corpus of 71,771 training samples derived from four public datasets, covering both Chinese and English scenarios. Each sample contains an overlapping speech mixture, per-speaker enrollment audio, a ground-truth transcript, and frame-level voice activity labels. Second, we propose a proxy-supervised joint training strategy that fine-tunes a BSRNN-based TSE model using four complementary differentiable objectives: ASR cross-entropy, speaker similarity, frame-level voice activity detection, and perceptual audio quality. Starting from a publicly available pre-trained checkpoint, only the BSRNN separator is updated during fine-tuning. On the REAL-T challenge leaderboard, PS4 ranks 2nd overall, achieving the best speaker similarity and timing F1 among all submitted systems.

</details>


### [179] [MuScriptor: An Open Model for Multi-Instrument Music Transcription](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08168)
*Simon Rouard,Michael Krause,Axel Roebel,Carl-Johann Simon-Gabriel,Alexandre Défossez*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MuScriptor combines synthetic pre-training, real-data fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning post-training with instrument presence conditioning to achieve robust multi-instrument music transcription on real-world recordings across diverse genres.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automatic music transcription methods are limited to single-instrument recordings or fail on complex real music mixes, and models trained on synthetic data generalize poorly to realistic multi-instrument settings.

Method: 1) Pre-training on synthetic data; 2) Fine-tuning on real music audio; 3) Post-training using reinforcement learning; 4) Conditioning on instrument presence for customizable transcriptions.

Result: MuScriptor works effectively on real-world music recordings across diverse musical genres, overcoming the poor generalization of prior synthetic-data-based approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed multi-stage training pipeline (synthetic pre-training → real-data fine-tuning → RL post-training) combined with instrument conditioning enables a practical, open-weight multi-instrument transcription model for real-world use.

Abstract: Existing methods for automatic music transcription are often limited to single-instrument recordings or fail on complex, real music mixes. Although previous work utilizes synthetic training data, the resulting models generalize poorly, leading to largely unusable transcription output in realistic, multi-instrument settings. In this work, we analyze the effectiveness of synthetic data for pre-training while combining it with fine-tuning on real music audio and post-training using reinforcement learning. We further introduce conditioning on instrument presence to customize transcriptions. Finally, we release MuScriptor, an open-weight multi-instrument music transcription model that works on real-world music recordings from across a diverse range of musical genres.

</details>


### [180] [A Quantized Native Runtime for On-Device Semantic Audio Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08526)
*Matteo Spanio,Antonio Rodà*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Aria is a dependency-free native runtime that runs Stable Audio 3's text-to-music pipeline on commodity hardware (GPUs, CPUs, Raspberry Pi 5) using quantization, achieving up to 8-bit precision with no quality loss and enabling activation steering for controllable generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semantic audio applications need controllable generation on commodity/embedded hardware without heavy datacenter frameworks, which existing implementations fail to provide.

Method: A dependency-free native runtime (no Python/ML frameworks) that uses quantization (8-bit, 4-bit) to reduce memory footprint, owns internal tensors to expose activation steering, and runs the full SA3 text-to-music pipeline on ordinary GPUs, CPUs, and Raspberry Pi 5.

Result: 8-bit quantization shows no measurable quality loss across three metrics (prompt adherence, audio quality, taste preservation) while cutting memory sharply and being fastest on GPU. 4-bit adds small bounded cost but fits the 1.2B model on 8GB Pi. Aria matches/exceeds official generation speed and starts ~7x faster. Steering interface enables controllable sonic seasoning.

Conclusion: Compact, quantized runtime with built-in control is practical for on-device semantic audio in Internet-of-Sounds settings.

Abstract: Semantic audio applications increasingly require controllable generation on commodity and embedded hardware rather than through framework-heavy datacenter stacks. We present \textit{aria}, a dependency-free native runtime that runs the complete text-to-music pipeline of Stable Audio~3 (SA3) on ordinary GPUs, CPU-only machines, and a Raspberry~Pi~5, with no Python or deep-learning framework underneath. Our main contribution is a study of quantization: running the model at lower numerical precision to fit tight memory budgets, saving memory in place rather than adding to it. Because the runtime owns every internal tensor, it also exposes activation steering, a low-cost way to steer what the model generates. We judge the quality cost with three independent measures of the output (prompt adherence, overall audio quality, taste preservation), each compared against the ordinary variation between random seeds. Eight-bit precision shows no measurable quality loss on any measure while sharply cutting memory, and it is the fastest mode on the GPU; four-bit adds a small, bounded cost but shrinks the footprint enough to run the $1.2$-billion-parameter model on an $8$\,GB Pi. Against the official implementation, aria matches or exceeds generation speed and starts about seven times faster. A case study of the steering interface generates music carrying taste associations (\emph{sonic seasoning}), with genuine but bounded control for a subset of attributes. These results make a compact, quantized runtime with built-in control a practical basis for on-device semantic audio in Internet-of-Sounds settings. The \textit{aria} runtime is released at https://github.com/matteospanio/aria.

</details>


### [181] [Structural Bottlenecks on Frequency Representation in End-to-End Audio Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08545)
*Nicole Cosme-Clifford*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: State-of-the-art strided convolutional encoders impose two structural bottlenecks (alias collapse and limited frequency resolution) that degrade access to interpretable frequency-localized primitives; the proposed Gabor Latent Refactorization (GLRF) is a lightweight post-hoc intervention that recovers frequency resolution and improves steerability without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether end-to-end neural audio encoders preserve access to interpretable features like pitch and timbre, as their high-fidelity performance might suggest, or whether they encode these features in entangled, non-interpretable bases.

Method: Theoretical analysis and controlled experiments identify two structural bottlenecks in strided convolutional encoders: (1) aliasing collapse into equivalence classes, and (2) limited frequency resolution from learned filters. Then introduces GLRF, a post-hoc intervention that re-expresses encoder latents in a Gabor (frequency-localized) basis.

Result: Collapse rates of 31-35% and filter bandwidths 10-35x above theoretical resolution bound under realistic conditions. GLRF reduces bandwidths to 1.5-3x of the bound while preserving reconstruction fidelity and improving control over attributes like pitch.

Conclusion: Strided convolutional encoders predictably degrade access to frequency-localized primitives, entangling downstream features. GLRF recovers much of that access without retraining, improving interpretability and steerability.

Abstract: End-to-end neural audio models achieve high-fidelity compression and generation. We might read that performance as evidence they directly represent interpretable features such as pitch and timbre, but a model can produce plausible outputs without doing so. A model may encode these features in any reachable basis, but regardless of which, the features are well described as compositions of time-frequency-localized primitives. Whether state-of-the-art encoders preserve access to these primitives, and thus to compositions of them, remains unclear. Through theoretical analysis and controlled experiments, we show that several state-of-the-art strided convolutional encoders impose two structural bottlenecks, both predictable from architecture and signal structure, on access to these primitives: (1) they collapse primitives into alias equivalence classes, establishing a bound on representational capacity, and (2) they limit the frequency resolution available to learned filters, restricting separability. For well structured data, we find collapse rates of 31-35% and filter bandwidths 10-35x above the theoretical resolution bound, confirming that both bottlenecks arise under realistic signal conditions. We then introduce Gabor Latent Refactorization (GLRF), a lightweight post-hoc intervention that re-expresses encoder latents in a frequency-localized basis, reducing filter bandwidths from 10-35x to 1.5-3x of the theoretical resolution bound while preserving reconstruction fidelity and improving control over attributes like pitch. These results show that the encoders in question predictably degrade access to frequency-localized primitives, entangling the features that depend on them, and that a lightweight, retraining-free intervention can recover much of that access, improving steerability and interpretability.

</details>


### [182] [It Takes Few to TANGO: A Quantized Distributed Model for Binaural Speech Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08645)
*Zahra Benslimane,Pierre Chouteau,Martyna Poreba,Fabrice Auzanneau,Michal Szczepanski,Fabian Chersi,Romain Serizel*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces MN-TANGO, a low-precision variant of the TANGO binaural speech enhancement system. It uses INT8 quantization (via post-training quantization and quantization-aware training) combined with ERB compression and grouped recurrent layers to drastically reduce computational (4.65 MMAC/s) and memory (0.177 MB) costs while maintaining performance, as the spatial filtering stage compensates for quantization-induced mask errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural network-based multichannel speech enhancement systems have high computational and memory demands that hinder deployment on resource-constrained devices.

Method: Applied post-training quantization and quantization-aware training to TANGO's neural mask estimators, analyzed error propagation through spatial filtering, and simplified the architecture into MN-TANGO using INT8 quantization, ERB compression, and grouped recurrent layers.

Result: The most compact MN-TANGO achieves 4.65 MMAC/s and 0.177 MB, with the spatial filtering stage compensating for most quantization-induced errors.

Conclusion: Low-precision inference combined with architectural simplifications enables efficient binaural speech enhancement on resource-constrained devices without significant performance degradation.

Abstract: Neural network-based multichannel speech enhancement systems achieve strong enhancement performance, but their computational and memory requirements limit deployment on resource-constrained devices. This paper investigates low-precision inference for TANGO, a hybrid distributed binaural speech enhancement system combining neural mask estimation with spatial filtering. We evaluate post-training quantization and quantization-aware training for the neural components, and analyze how quantization errors in the mask estimators propagate through the downstream spatial filtering stage. Our analysis shows that, although quantization degrades intermediate mask estimates, the spatial filtering stage compensates for most quantization-induced errors. Leveraging this robustness, we simplify TANGO into MN-TANGO, reducing both model size and computational complexity while maintaining comparable final performance. By combining INT8 weight-and-activation quantization with ERB compression and grouped recurrent layers, the most compact MN-TANGO reaches 4.65 MMAC/s and 0.177 MB.

</details>


### [183] [MulTTiPop: A Multitrack Transcription Dataset for Pop Music](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08756)
*Nathan Pruyne,Benjamin Stoler,William Chen,Chien-yu Huang,Shinji Watanabe,Chris Donahue*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MulTTiPop is a 572-segment, 3.5-hour pop music dataset (1930s-2000s) with multitrack MIDI alignments, created via metadata matching and beat-synchronized warping. SOTA AMT models achieve only 38% Onset F1, indicating significant room for improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a benchmark dataset of pop music with aligned multitrack MIDI recordings for evaluating automatic music transcription (AMT) models, addressing the lack of diverse, real-world pop music evaluation data.

Method: 1) Metadata-based matching on song segments from Lakh MIDI and TheoryTab datasets; 2) Manual identification of an anchor beat between audio and MIDI; 3) Beat tracking on audio and warping the MIDI to match tempo and timing.

Result: The best-performing AMT model achieves only 38% Onset F1 on MulTTiPop, revealing substantial room for improvement in current transcription systems.
