Result: Demonstrated basic application with MNIST, evaluated on visual question answering (Clevr) and multi-object tracking (MOT) benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a practical platform for ASP-centric robust end-to-end training in dynamic domains, validated on perception and interaction tasks.

Abstract: We present a general neurosymbolic reasoning and learning methodology based on a modular integration of answer set programming with an energy based model substrate. Key contributions are: (1) supporting joint optimisation in the continuous latent space through explicit ASP-based declarative semantics fully incorporating background knowledge, constraints, non-monotonic inference; and (2) advancing recent works at the interface of answer sets, probabilistic logic, and answer set modulo theories by providing a generalised model and practical platform for ASP-centric robust, end-to-end training for applications in dynamic domains (e.g., involving perception and interaction). We provide a practical implementation, and demonstrate basic use and application (with MNIST), and evaluate with the visual question-answering benchmark Clevr and the multi-object tracking benchmark MOT.

</details>


### [210] [Overthinking: Amplifying Reasoning Weights to Extract Learned Secrets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08173)
*Jack Hopkins,Dipika Khullar,Fabien Roger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces "overthinking" — amplifying reasoning model vectors beyond the standard reasoning model to make language models more likely to reveal hidden information during auditing, showing up to 10× increase in secret surfacing across 2B-32B models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-box auditing may miss subtle misalignment and hidden information; need better methods to elicit hidden information during auditing.

Method: Define overthinking model as θ_𝒪_α = θ_ℳ + α(θ_ℛ - θ_ℳ) with α>1, using reasoning task vectors from a reasoning-distilled model. Also introduce layer-wise attenuation strategies to selectively amplify reasoning while maintaining output quality.

Result: Overthinking models reveal hidden information up to 10× more frequently than the original reasoning model across four experimental settings and model sizes 2B-32B. Different secret types require different perturbation approaches.

Conclusion: Reasoning amplification (overthinking) is an effective tool for surfacing secrets or unintended behaviors acquired during training, useful for pre-deployment auditing.

Abstract: Black box auditing of language models is an essential pre-deployment tool, but it may miss subtle forms of misalignment and hidden information. To better elicit hidden information during an auditing process, we introduce \emph{overthinking}: the process of using reasoning task vectors to amplify the propensity to think out loud of reasoning models. Given the parameters of a non-reasoning instruct model $M$ and reasoning-distilled model $R$, we define the \emph{overthinking model} as $\boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{O}_α} = \boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{M}} + α(\boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{R}} - \boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{M}})$, where $α> 1$ amplifies reasoning beyond the pure reasoning model $R$. Additionally, we introduce new layer-wise attenuation strategies that selectively amplify reasoning without losing quality and coherence of model outputs. We demonstrate that overthinking models are more likely to reveal hidden information across four experimental settings, across 2B-32B models. Our findings suggest that reasoning amplification may surface secrets or unintended behaviors acquired during training up to $10\times$ more frequently than the original reasoning model. How secrets surface depends on the secret type: some require perturbation along the reasoning direction, while others yield to any sufficiently large weight perturbation.

</details>


### [211] [ASMR: Agentic Schema Generation for Ship Maintenance Report Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08177)
*Sohrab Namazi Nia,Amogh Dalal,Ning Sa,Peter Ly,Marti Zentmaier,Tomek Strzalkowski,Jay Miller,Rishi Singh,Senjuti Basu Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASMR is a modular agentic framework with two agents (Field Generation Agent and Structural Optimizer Agent) that uses adaptive multi-granularity clustering and reinforcement learning to automatically discover compact, informative schemas from historical ship maintenance and operational reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the automatic schema generation problem for historical ship maintenance and operational reports across multiple form categories, enabling the discovery of compact and informative schemas that capture essential information requirements for each report type.

Method: ASMR framework with two agents: (1) Field Generation Agent extracts semantic concepts from narratives and generates candidate schema fields via adaptive multi-granularity clustering; (2) Structural Optimizer Agent uses reinforcement learning to identify compact, informative, non-redundant schema representations.

Result: Preliminary results demonstrate the promise of the proposed approach and highlight open research challenges at the intersection of data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

Conclusion: The resulting schemas can guide report authors toward producing more complete, consistent, and actionable reports, with identified open challenges in data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the automatic schema generation problem: given a collection of historical ship maintenance and operational reports across multiple form categories, automatically discover compact and informative schemas that capture the essential information requirements of each report type. To address this challenge, we propose ASMR, a modular agentic framework consisting of two specialized agents. A Field Generation Agent extracts semantic concepts from historical narratives and generates candidate schema fields through adaptive multi-granularity clustering, while a Structural Optimizer Agent employs reinforcement learning to identify compact, informative, and non-redundant schema representations. The resulting schemas can guide report authors toward producing more complete, consistent, and actionable reports. Preliminary results demonstrate the promise of the proposed approach and highlight several open research challenges at the intersection of data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

</details>


### [212] [A First-Principles Theory of Slow Thinking and Active Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08196)
*Hongkang Yang,Zhi-Qin John Xu,Feiyu Xiong,Weinan E*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes "active lifting" theory that mathematically formalizes thinking and perception as probability distribution lifting/projection on observable and latent spaces, deriving slow thinking models for LLMs from first principles, with implications for representation hierarchies, encoder construction, and visual representation formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a first-principles mathematical formulation of thinking and perception that unifies the design, training, and inference of slow thinking large language models, and to formally derive active perception from fundamental probabilistic concepts.

Method: Lifting and projection of probability distributions on observable and latent spaces; active lifting theory based on sampling latent sequences and an intrinsic drive to reduce uncertainty with maximum rate; static theory subspace derivation; representation hierarchy and sampler hierarchy construction.

Result: Derivation of a large design space containing slow thinking models; position of models on representation and sampler hierarchies; inference process with internal time axis; training objective resembling minimum-length coding and language invention; characterization of agency of perception and emergence of slow thinking formats; three-stage pathway for improving slow thinking models; unified approach to encoders/generative models; a priori human-like visual representations; possible solution to policy collapse.

Conclusion: Active lifting provides a principled mathematical foundation for understanding and improving slow thinking in LLMs, with broad technical by-products including improved encoder construction, generative modeling across modalities, and insights into human-like perception and policy stability.

Abstract: As part of a series on first-principles modeling of cognitive functions, this paper attempts to provide a mathematical formulation of thinking and perception. It formally derives slow thinking or more generally, active perception, and encompasses the design, training and inference of slow thinking large language models. Our starting point is the lifting and projection of probability distributions on the observable and latent spaces, with the objective of representing complex data distributions by simple function families such as neural networks. A theory called "active lifting" is proposed, based on the sampling of latent sequences and an intrinsic drive to reduce uncertainty with maximum rate. It derives a large design space, containing the slow thinking models in a subspace that we call the static theory. These models are positioned on the representation hierarchy and sampler hierarchy induced by the static theory, and can be upgraded by climbing the two hierarchies. Active lifting further derives an inference process with an internal time axis, and a training objective that resembles minimum-length coding as well as the invention of languages. Thus, it characterizes the agency of perception, including the emergence of the slow thinking formats. Technical by-products of this theory include a three-stage pathway for improving slow thinking models, a unified approach to constructing encoders and generative models for all data modalities, a priori formation of human-like visual representations, and a possible solution to policy collapse.

</details>


### [213] [Playing ZendoWorld: Challenging AI Agents on Active Visual Concept Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08233)
*Sophia Koehler,Antonia Wüst,Inga Ibs,Wasu Top Piriyakulkij,Wolfgang Stammer,Constantin Rothkopf,Kevin Ellis,Kristian Kersting*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ZendoWorld is a benchmark for evaluating agents' ability to jointly perceive, hypothesize, and experiment. Key findings: (1) high label accuracy ≠ rule recovery; (2) perception vs. induction bottlenecks vary by agent class; (3) VLM agents propose uninformative experiments, failing active learning. Humans outperform all agents but still struggle with complex rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study and evaluate intelligent agents' ability to jointly perceive complex inputs, form hypotheses about hidden patterns, and design informative experiments—a central challenge in building intelligent systems, particularly relevant to scientific discovery.

Method: Introduced ZendoWorld, a controlled interactive environment where agents infer logical rules from visual game observations, propose new scenes to acquire information, and refine hypotheses based on feedback. Evaluated agents spanning pure VLM reasoning, Bayesian particle filtering, dynamic concept discovery, and neuro-symbolic methods, with human data collection for comparison.

Result: (1) High label prediction accuracy does not imply rule recovery; (2) perception and induction present distinct bottlenecks for different agent classes; (3) VLM-based agents propose near-uninformative experiments, failing to reduce hypothesis uncertainty. Humans outperformed all agents but still showed gaps in inductive reasoning for complex rules.

Conclusion: ZendoWorld serves as an important benchmark for evaluating intelligent agents, identifying concrete avenues for improvement in inductive reasoning and active experimentation, particularly applicable to scientific discovery domains.

Abstract: A central challenge in building intelligent systems is enabling agents to jointly perceive complex inputs, form hypotheses about hidden patterns, and design informative experiments to test them. To study this problem, we propose ZendoWorld, a controlled interactive environment in which agents must infer a logical rule about visual game observations, acquire information by proposing new scenes, and refine their hypotheses based on feedback from the game environment. We evaluate several agents spanning pure VLM reasoning, Bayesian particle filtering, dynamic concept discovery, and neuro-symbolic methods. Our main findings are: (1) high accuracy in predicting labels for observed examples does not imply recovery of the underlying rule; (2) perception and induction are distinct bottlenecks for different agent classes; and (3) VLM-based agents propose near-uninformative experiments, failing to actively reduce hypothesis uncertainty. To compare these results, we collect human data on the task, which reveals a gap in inductive reasoning, particularly for more complex rules. Overall, ZENDOWORLD takes an important step toward evaluating intelligent agents and identifies concrete avenues for improvement, particularly in domains like scientific discovery.

</details>


### [214] [AutoPersonas: A Multi-Timescale Loop Engine for Open-Ended Persona Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08252)
*Mengchen Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoPersonas introduces a multi-timescale life-environment engine (OSO loop) that separates divergence from evidence-governed absorption to reduce persona self-locking—where agents collapse into repetitive, stale behaviors—while preserving identity continuity, demonstrated through compressed simulations, stress tests, and A/B experiments showing reduced macro-theme repetition (61.8%→36.3%) and doubled cumulative theme count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term persona agents face self-locking: a runtime failure where locally plausible events keep occurring but the generated life collapses toward familiar environments, weak relationships, suspended decisions, and stale life stages, caused by model-level convergence to high-probability channels and system-level context gravity.

Method: AutoPersonas is a multi-timescale life-environment engine that separates environment-side Occurrences, accumulated Observations, and persona State. Its OSO (Occurrence-State-Observation) loop admits divergent future-facing material while requiring evidence-governed absorption before State or reachability changes.

Result: Eight-model 40-day stress test: mean rolling 5-day action-category repetition 95.2%-97.6%, all models crossing 90% by day 11. Semantic re-keeping: 79.0%-88.0% macro-theme repetition. A/B test: context-slice masking + per-sample divergence targeting reduced macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3%, roughly doubling cumulative theme count.

Conclusion: Separating controlled divergence from evidence-governed absorption can reduce persona-environment self-locking while preserving identity continuity, supporting the bounded claim that this approach is effective for long-term persona agents.

Abstract: Long-term persona agents must remain identifiable while adapting to new events, relationships, evidence, and social conditions. We identify self-locking as a runtime failure mode in continuing persona-life loops: locally plausible events keep appearing while the generated life collapses toward familiar environments, weak relationships, suspended decisions, and stale life stages. We trace this failure to model-level convergence toward high-probability behavioral channels and system-level context gravity from State, memory, history, and environment summaries. We introduce AutoPersonas, a multi-timescale life-environment engine for bounded persona-level recursive self-evolution. It separates environment-side Occurrences, accumulated Observations, and persona State. Its OSO loop admits divergent future-facing material while requiring evidence-governed absorption before State or reachability changes. A three-year compressed simulation exposed environment watermark shells, occurrence-hardening gaps, slow-change accumulation failures, recursive indecision, and weak relationship persistence. An eight-model 40-day stress test generated 1,600 events and found mean rolling 5-day action-category repetition of 95.2%-97.6%, with all models crossing 90% by day 11. Semantic re-keeping found 79.0%-88.0% macro-theme repetition across all direct-loop runs. In a same-runtime 40-day A/B, context-slice masking plus per-sample divergence targeting reduced macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3% and roughly doubled cumulative theme count. A juvenile-goblin fictional-world run reproduced the anti-fixation regime without hard real-world intrusions. These results support a bounded claim: separating controlled divergence from evidence-governed absorption can reduce persona-environment self-locking while preserving identity continuity.

</details>


### [215] [Compete Then Collaborate: Frontier AI Teachers Build a Verifiable Curriculum to Improve a Coding Student Beyond Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08255)
*Miseong Shawn Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Four frontier AI teachers (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) are ranked via execution-based judge, then collaborate to build a verifiable curriculum for a student model. Imitation (SFT) on teacher solutions fails, but using the curriculum as an RLVR environment improves student performance significantly. Key insight: teacher collaboration value is in constructing verifiable environments for learning-by-doing, not pooling answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-teacher knowledge distillation merges outputs without determining which teacher teaches best, often relying on biased LLM judges. This paper seeks to establish fair teacher ranking via execution verification and explore whether teacher collaboration can effectively improve student models.

Method: Compete-then-collaborate framework: (1) Four frontier teachers (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) compete via head-to-head execution-based judge (unit tests, stdin-stdout checks) with fairness controls. (2) Teachers collaborate to build a verifiable curriculum for Qwen2.5-Coder student. Experiments compare SFT on verified solutions vs. RLVR using the collaborative curriculum.

Result: (1) All teachers saturate at 99-100% on standard problems after self-correction; harder competition problems separate them (Gemini 77% > Claude 69% = Codex 69% > Grok 50%), but student-side results don't depend on teacher ranking. (2) SFT on verified solutions degrades student performance (e.g., 76.7%→72.7% on MBPP-test, 5.9%→2.9% on competition problems). (3) RLVR on the collaborative curriculum improves student (5.9%→8.8% peak on competition, +49% relative), reversing SFT's negative direction.

Conclusion: The value of AI-teacher collaboration is not in pooling answers for imitation, but in jointly constructing a verifiable environment where the student learns by doing (RLVR). SFT can harm already-competent students while RLVR yields meaningful gains.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly serve as teachers generating training data for smaller students. Prior multi-teacher knowledge distillation methods merge outputs without determining which frontier model teaches best, often relying on an LLM judge biased toward its own outputs. We introduce a compete-then-collaborate framework where four frontier AI teachers (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) are ranked head-to-head by an execution-based judge (unit tests and stdin-stdout checks) with fairness controls, and then collaborate to build a verifiable curriculum for a student (Qwen2.5-Coder). We report three findings. (1) Under execution verification, all teachers solve standard problems near-perfectly after self-correction (99-100%) due to a saturation effect, but harder competition problems separate them (Gemini 77% > Claude 69% = Codex 69% > Grok 50%); however, the robust student-side results do not depend on teacher ranking. (2) Imitation (SFT) on verified solutions does not improve, and can degrade, an already-competent student at 7B and 32B (e.g., from 76.7% to 72.7% on MBPP-test, and 5.9% to 2.9% on competition problems). (3) Using the same collaborative curriculum as a reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) environment improves the student (from 5.9% to 8.8% peak on competition problems, a +49% relative gain), reversing SFT's direction. The value of AI-teacher collaboration lies not in pooling answers to imitate, but in jointly constructing a verifiable environment where the student learns by doing. We release a reproducible on-prem pipeline (NVIDIA GB10) with framework patches for running GRPO on a bleeding-edge stack.

</details>


### [216] [MentalHospital: A Virtual Environment for Evaluating Psychiatric Clinical Encounters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08257)
*Yuming Yang,Xiao Sun,Yuanwei Zou,Zhengxiao Wu,Yun Chen,Jiang Zhong,Haoyang Zeng,Jingwang Huang,Kaiwen Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MentalHospital is a virtual evaluation environment simulating complete psychiatric clinical encounters (S.O.A.P. workflow) using 1,193 EHR-based patient cases and a dual-track evaluation protocol. The companion MentalEval framework uses five rubric-grounded specialist evaluators to assess LLM performance, revealing that even the best LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 points, with mental status assessment being the key bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate LLMs on isolated psychiatric tasks but fail to simulate complete clinical encounters, leaving a gap in assessing holistic psychiatric competence.

Method: MentalHospital instantiates the S.O.A.P. workflow with skill-augmented standardized patients from 1,193 de-identified EHR cases (76 disorders across ICD-11 categories). Evaluation uses a dual-track protocol combining objective EHR-derived reference comparison with subjective clinical process quality assessment. MentalEval uses five domain-specific evaluators (communication empathy, interviewing professionalism, clinical-note quality, diagnostic rigor, treatment appropriateness) trained with rubric-grounded SFT and expert-guided DPO.

Result: 22 clinicians rated MentalHospital's clinical fidelity at 3.88/5. MentalEval achieved strong expert alignment with average QWK of 0.944. The strongest LLM trailed clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence.

Conclusion: Current LLMs significantly underperform clinicians in complete psychiatric encounters, with mental status assessment identified as the primary bottleneck, highlighting the need for more comprehensive evaluation and training in psychiatric AI.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on isolated psychiatric tasks, including dialogue, diagnosis, and treatment planning, yet existing benchmarks rarely simulate complete psychiatric clinical encounters. We introduce $\textbf{MentalHospital}$, a virtual evaluation environment for LLM-based psychiatric clinical encounters. MentalHospital instantiates the Subjective Interviewing, Objective Examination, Diagnostic Assessment, and Treatment Planning (S.O.A.P.) workflow, using skill-augmented standardized patients constructed from 1,193 de-identified psychiatric electronic health record (EHR) cases spanning all major ICD-11 categories and 76 disorders. Each encounter is assessed through a dual-track protocol that combines objective comparison against EHR-derived references with subjective assessment of clinical process quality. To scale specialist judgment, we develop $\textbf{MentalEval}$, five domain-specific evaluators covering communication empathy, interviewing professionalism, clinical-note quality, diagnostic rigor, and treatment appropriateness, trained with rubric-grounded SFT and expert-guided DPO. Survey responses from 22 clinicians support MentalHospital's clinical fidelity (3.88/5), while MentalEval achieves strong expert alignment with an average QWK of 0.944. Benchmarking shows that even the strongest LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence, with mental status assessment as a key bottleneck.

</details>


### [217] [Different Teachers, Different Capabilities: Sub-1B On-Device Distillation for Structured Text Enrichment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08268)
*Vinay Kumar Chaganti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Distilling a reasoning teacher (DeepSeek-R1:8B) into a small student (Qwen3-0.6B) recovers 58% of the teacher-student quality gap on structured extraction tasks, running 48× faster (0.8s vs 39s per article), but the student is less faithful on thin-source articles; a per-field routing map is proposed since no single engine dominates all sub-tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-volume structured extraction incurs high latency/cost when using large models on every item; distillation into a small on-device model offers comparable output at a fraction of the time and cost.

Method: Distill an 8B reasoning teacher (DeepSeek-R1:8B) into a 0.6B student (Qwen3-0.6B) via QLoRA across three seeds; compare against a same-size non-reasoning teacher, a larger managed pipeline, and two baselines (few-shot prompting, constrained decoding). Evaluate with a blinded, reference-free three-judge panel scoring each arm against the full article on a 93-item test set.

Result: Student runs 0.8s/article vs teacher's 39s; recovers 58% of the base-to-teacher gap on summary quality; beats constrained decoding by +16.8 points and few-shot prompting by +4.9. Reasoning teacher transfers writing quality; managed pipeline transfers label diversity. Student from reasoning teacher fabricates on thin-source articles (74 vs 55 faithful). Same-size non-reasoning teacher yields student no better than untuned base.

Conclusion: No single engine wins every field; the practical deliverable is a per-field routing map for on-device enrichment, where different teacher types (reasoning, managed pipeline, instruction) suit different sub-tasks (writing quality, label diversity, groundedness).

Abstract: High-volume structured extraction pays a large model's latency on every item, so distilling the task into a small on-device model is attractive: comparable output at a fraction of the time and cost. We measure what that distillation actually delivers, per sub-task. Each news article is mapped to one JSON object with a short summary and five categorical labels. We distill an 8B reasoning teacher (deepseek-r1:8b) into a 0.6B student (Qwen3-0.6B; QLoRA, three seeds), and add two teacher controls: a same-size non-reasoning teacher and a larger managed pipeline. A blinded, reference-free, three-judge panel scores every arm against the full article, alongside two non-distillation baselines, few-shot prompting and constrained decoding. The student runs at about 0.8 s per article against the teacher's 39 s, and recovers 58% of the base-to-teacher gap on summary quality, beating its primary baseline (constrained decoding) by +16.8 points and few-shot prompting by a secondary +4.9. A same-size non-reasoning teacher trains a student no better than the untuned base, so the summary gain follows from the teacher's reasoning nature rather than its scale. Capabilities then split by teacher: the reasoning teacher transfers writing quality and the managed pipeline transfers label diversity, while a same-size instruction teacher's students stay more grounded on the 22 short, thin-source articles in the 93-item test set (74 versus 55 faithful), where the reasoning-lineage student fabricates. That grounding difference is a consistent ordering rather than a significant aggregate effect, and the subgroup is small, so we report it as a direction. Because no single engine wins every field, the deliverable is a per-field routing map for on-device enrichment.

</details>


### [218] [PolyUQuest: Verifiable Structure-Aware Web RAG over Heterogeneous Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08269)
*Ying Liu,Yi Ye,Quanyu Feng,Mingxi Ye,Mingtao Zhang,Haoyang Li,Chen Jason Zhang,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PolyUQuest is a structure-aware web RAG framework that uses a heterogeneous graph (hyperlinks, DOM hierarchy, entities) with a two-tier router for three retrieval modes, enabling verifiable answers with traced evidence, outperforming flat-text RAG systems on a PolyU website benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG systems treat web pages as flat text, losing structural and semantic signals encoded in HTML, which limits answer correctness, coverage, and verifiability.

Method: Builds a heterogeneous graph unifying hyperlink topology, DOM hierarchy, and entity-relation knowledge; uses a two-tier router to dispatch queries to one of three retrieval modes: direct block retrieval, cross-page graph traversal, and multi-hop entity reasoning.

Result: Outperforms existing RAG systems in answer correctness, coverage, and faithfulness while consuming significantly fewer LLM tokens per query on a benchmark of 4,240 pages, 31,086 DOM blocks, 29,119 entities, and 37,680 relations.

Conclusion: Structure-aware web RAG with heterogeneous graph and routing improves verifiability and efficiency; being deployed as a student-facing QA service at PolyU.

Abstract: Existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems treat web pages as flat text, losing the structural and semantic signals encoded in HTML. We present PolyUQuest, a verifiable, structure-aware web RAG framework built on a heterogeneous graph that unifies hyperlink topology between pages, DOM hierarchy within pages, and entity-relation knowledge across pages. A two-tier router dispatches each query to one of three retrieval modes matched to its structural need, including direct block retrieval, cross-page graph traversal, and multi-hop entity reasoning. Every answer is fully verifiable, as each cited block carries its source page, heading path, and entity links so that users can trace any claim back to its structural evidence. We evaluate on the official websites of the Hong Kong Polytechnic University (PolyU), comprising 4,240 pages, 31,086 DOM blocks, 29,119 entities, and 37,680 relations, together with a multi-type evaluation benchmark. PolyUQuest outperforms existing RAG systems in answer correctness, coverage, and faithfulness, while consuming significantly fewer LLM tokens per query. The demonstration provides an interactive interface for inspecting cited answers, comparing retrieval traces across routing modes, and exploring evidence graph paths. PolyUQuest is being prepared for deployment as a student-facing QA service at PolyU.

</details>


### [219] [Understanding Axes of Difficulty For Long Context Tasks Via PredicateLongBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08284)
*Siddhartha Jain,Ameya Velingker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PredicateLongBench stress-tests LLMs' long-context reasoning by requiring identification of the longest contiguous subsequence satisfying given predicates, exploring multiple axes of difficulty through both synthetic and real-world pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-context evaluations measure average-case performance, are often saturated or lack robustness, and lack systematic ways to probe model performance as task difficulty scales across various axes.

Method: The benchmark asks models to find the longest contiguous subsequence in long inputs that satisfies given predicates (e.g., lexicographic ordering). It provides two generation pipelines: fully synthetic with random word-like strings, and real-world sampling from natural documents while preserving distributional properties. Multiple axes of difficulty are systematically explored.

Result: Frontier models struggle as task difficulty scales along the defined axes, demonstrating the benchmark's utility in revealing limitations of current long-context capabilities.

Conclusion: PredicateLongBench fills a gap in long-context evaluation by providing a robust, systematic stress-test that is conceptually simple, does not require LLM-based generations or judges, and exposes shortcomings in current models' long-context understanding.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated rapidly improving long-context capabilities, prompting a wave of benchmarks designed to evaluate them. However, existing long-context evaluations - from Needle-in-a-Haystack (NIAH) tests to more recent multi-hop reasoning and summarization tasks - predominantly measure average-case performance, and many are either saturated or lack robustness. Notably absent is a systematic way to probe how models perform as we scale up the difficulty of tasks along various axes. We address this gap by proposing PredicateLongBench, a benchmark that stress-tests long-context reasoning by asking models to identify the longest contiguous subsequence of words in a long input that satisfies given predicates/constraints (e.g., lexicographic ordering), drawn from a broader predicate class. The central innovation of our benchmark is the identification and systematic exploration of multiple different axes of difficulty which test multiple aspects of long context understanding. We provide two complementary generation pipelines - a fully synthetic setup using random word-like strings, and a real-world setup that samples words from natural documents while preserving their distributional properties. We find that frontier models struggle to perform well as we scale up the difficulty of tasks along our axes, demonstrating the utility of our benchmark in understanding the limitations of current long-context capabilities. Furthermore, the tasks in PredicateLongBench, though challenging, are conceptually simple and do not require LLM-based generations or judges.

</details>


### [220] [Psychological Competence as a Missing Dimension in AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08285)
*Marcos Economides,Paul M. Sacher,Samuel Salzer,Alexis Michelle Abellar,Fendi Tsim,Antoine Ferrère*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "psychological competence" as a missing dimension in AI evaluation for human-facing systems, arguing that current frameworks focused on technical performance are insufficient for AI systems that act as advisors, coaches, tutors, and companions. The authors define psychological competence as an AI's capacity to support user cognition, emotional interpretation, and decision-making through appropriate interaction properties (framing, tone, authority, responsiveness, uncertainty handling, conversational guidance), and outline assessment methods including scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, and model-assisted evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI evaluation frameworks focus on technical performance (accuracy, robustness, reasoning, policy compliance) but are insufficient for human-facing AI systems that interact directly with users through natural language, as these systems can shape how users reason, interpret emotions, form beliefs, calibrate trust, and make decisions.

Method: The paper develops a conceptual framework for psychological competence by drawing on behavioral science and human-AI interaction research, defining core domains and clarifying the construct's boundaries. It proposes assessment through scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, and model-assisted evaluation methods rather than a specific benchmark.

Result: The paper identifies that existing evaluation approaches capture parts of the problem but rarely assess psychological effects directly. It defines psychological competence and its core interaction properties including framing, tone, perceived authority, responsiveness, uncertainty handling, and conversational guidance.

Conclusion: Psychological competence should become a core consideration for model providers, deploying organizations, researchers, and regulators concerned with the real-world effects of human-facing AI systems, as the relevant unit of evaluation is not only the model but the human-AI interaction.

Abstract: Current AI evaluation frameworks focus primarily on technical performance, including accuracy, robustness, reasoning ability, and policy compliance. These measures remain essential, but they are not sufficient for systems that interact directly with users through natural language. Human-facing AI systems are increasingly used as advisors, coaches, tutors, and companions. In these roles, their responses can shape how users reason, interpret emotions, form beliefs, calibrate trust, and make decisions. The relevant unit of evaluation is therefore not only the model, but the human-AI interaction. This paper introduces psychological competence as a missing dimension in AI evaluation. We define psychological competence as the capacity of a human-facing AI system to support user cognition, emotional interpretation, and behavioral decision-making in ways that are appropriate to the user, context, and purpose of the interaction. This includes interaction properties such as framing, tone, perceived authority, responsiveness, uncertainty handling, and conversational guidance. Existing evaluation approaches capture parts of this problem but rarely assess these psychological effects directly. Drawing on behavioral science and human-AI interaction research, we outline a conceptual framework for psychological competence and its core domains. Rather than proposing a specific benchmark, we define the construct, clarify its boundaries, and describe how it may be assessed through scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, and model-assisted evaluation methods. We argue that psychological competence should become a core consideration for model providers, deploying organizations, researchers, and regulators concerned with the real-world effects of human-facing AI systems.

</details>


### [221] [INTENT: An LSTM Framework for Vehicle Intention Prediction in Intersection Scenarios with Comprehensive Ablation Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08316)
*Logine M. Zaki,Catherine M. Elias*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes INTENT, an LSTM-based framework for predicting vehicle intentions (straight, left turn, right turn) at intersections 2 seconds ahead, achieving 99.71% accuracy on the InD dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vehicle intention prediction is critical for autonomous vehicle safety and agility, especially in complex driving scenarios (intersections, roundabouts, emergency stops) where human-like interpretation of driver intent enables timely evasive actions to prevent or minimize catastrophic outcomes.

Method: The INTENT framework uses an LSTM (Long Short-Term Memory) model to predict vehicle intention at intersections with a 2-second anticipation horizon, classifying three maneuvers: going straight, turning left, or turning right.

Result: The model achieved 99.71% accuracy on the InD dataset, validated through various model experiments and ablation studies.

Conclusion: The INTENT framework demonstrates that LSTM-based models can achieve highly accurate vehicle intention prediction at intersections, which can also serve as a foundation for intention-conditioned trajectory prediction to further enhance autonomous driving safety.

Abstract: Vehicle intention prediction is a pivotal aspect in the agility and safety of autonomous vehicles in all driving scenarios; if genuine enhancement of autonomous vehicles are required, we need to make them adopt human interpretation of driver's intention especially in cases that require a lot of human interaction as well as complex driving behaviors like the ones at intersections, roundabouts and emergency cases such as sudden stops where vehicle intention prediction helps in taking the correct evasive action within a real time period where every second of action makes an impact and can prevent a catastrophe from taking place. In the worst case, it helps minimize the damage and make safety a priority. Intention prediction can also be used to enhance trajectory prediction (intention conditioned trajectory prediction). In this study, The INTENT framework is proposed using LSTM model to predict the vehicle's intention at intersections 2 seconds ahead of the event occurrence to predict whether the cars in intersections are going straight, turning left, or turning right. Various model experiments and ablation study are thoroughly tested on InD dataset achieving 99.71% accuracy.

</details>


### [222] [Blind-Spots-Bench: Evaluating Blind Spots in Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08317)
*Matteo Santelmo,Xiuying Wei,Israa Fakih,Felix Bauer,Juan Garcia Giraldo,Chengkun Li,Etienne Bamas,Emmanuel Abbé*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces blind-spots-bench, a benchmark of 235 human-simple but AI-hard tasks, collected from AI course students, to expose blind spots in modern models. Evaluation shows closed-source models outperform open-weight ones by ~10%, and no single model excels across all task types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks may under-measure persistent blind spots in current AI systems, as models still fail at tasks humans find trivial (e.g., string manipulation, drawing a five-legged dog).

Method: The authors collected raw questions from AI course students, cleaned and annotated them with structured reference solutions, and proposed a task taxonomy for the resulting 235 samples. They developed an automated grading pipeline to evaluate open-weight and closed-source language, vision-language, and image-generation models.

Result: Closed-source frontier models outperform open-weight models by ~10% despite comparable performance on existing benchmarks. No single model dominates across all task types; some tasks remain challenging for all evaluated models.

Conclusion: Blind-spots-bench serves as a diagnostic stress test for identifying concrete weaknesses in current AI models, highlighting gaps not captured by standard benchmarks.

Abstract: Modern AI models achieve strong performance on many established benchmarks, yet they still fail on tasks that humans find almost trivial, such as manipulating a string or drawing a dog with five legs. These examples suggest that existing benchmarks may under-measure persistent blind spots in current systems. We introduce $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$, a benchmark designed to expose such blind spots through tasks that appear simple for humans but remain challenging for modern AI. We collect raw questions from students in an AI course, clean and annotate them with structured reference solutions, and propose a task taxonomy tailored to the resulting dataset of 235 samples. We further develop an automated grading pipeline to evaluate a wide range of models, including open-weight and closed-source language, vision-language, and image-generation models. Our analysis on $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$ reveals that closed-source frontier models can substantially outperform open-weight models with even $\approx10\%$ gap, even when they attain comparable performance on existing benchmarks. A more fine-grained analysis shows that no single model dominates across all task types, and that some tasks remain challenging for all evaluated models. These results highlight the value of $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$ as a diagnostic stress test for identifying concrete weaknesses in current modern models.

</details>


### [223] [MobiDiff: Semantic-Aware Multi-Channel Discrete Diffusion for Human Mobility Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08357)
*Rongchao Xu,Lin Jiang,Dahai Yu,Ximiao Li,Taichi Liu,Desheng Zhang,Yuan Tian,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MobiDiff is an end-to-end discrete diffusion framework that generates synthetic human mobility data by denoising multi-channel semantic skeletons (spatial, activity, temporal), outperforming prior methods in efficiency (5.3× faster than GeoGen) while preserving trajectory statistics on real-world datasets from Atlanta, Boston, and Seattle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world mobility data are costly to collect and difficult to share due to privacy concerns, and existing diffusion-based methods rely on continuous or latent spatio-temporal traces that cannot natively model discrete semantic events with explicit region, activity, time, and interval structures.

Method: MobiDiff decomposes each human check-in event into spatial, activity, and temporal channels, and employs structured event-, group-, and channel-level masking to jointly capture trajectory-level mobility patterns and within-event dependencies, directly denoising multi-channel semantic skeletons without costly interpolation or coarse-to-fine pipelines.

Result: On three large-scale real-world datasets (Atlanta, Boston, Seattle), MobiDiff preserves trajectory length and temporal interval distributions while remaining competitive across broader mobility statistics, and is 5.3× faster than GeoGen on average during inference.

Conclusion: Discrete diffusion offers an interpretable and efficient framework for synthetic mobility data generation.

Abstract: Human mobility data are essential for transportation optimization, urban planning, and resource allocation, yet real-world mobility data are costly to collect and difficult to share due to privacy concerns. Recent diffusion-based methods have shown promise in synthesizing realistic mobility patterns, but they typically rely on continuous or latent spatio-temporal traces, limiting their ability to natively model discrete semantic events with explicit region, activity, time, and interval structures. To address this issue, we introduce MobiDiff, an end-to-end discrete diffusion framework that efficiently generates mobility data by directly denoising multi-channel semantic skeletons, avoiding the costly interpolation, latent trace construction, and coarse-to-fine realization pipelines widely used in existing diffusion-based methods. Specifically, MobiDiff decomposes each human check-in event into spatial, activity, and temporal channels, and employs structured event-, group-, and channel-level masking to jointly capture trajectory-level mobility patterns and within-event dependencies. We evaluate generation fidelity, privacy-preserving, and efficiency on three large-scale real-world datasets from Atlanta, Boston, and Seattle. Results show that MobiDiff effectively preserves trajectory length and temporal interval distributions while remaining competitive across broader mobility statistics; it is also much faster than state-of-the-art methods, e.g., 5.3$\times$ faster than GeoGen on average during inference. These findings suggest that discrete diffusion offers an interpretable and efficient framework for synthetic mobility data generation.

</details>


### [224] [FedOPAL: One-Shot Federated Learning via Analytic Visual Prompt Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08368)
*Lingyu Qiu,Daniela Annunziata,Stefano Izzo,Fabio Giampaolo,Francesco Piccialli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedOPAL uses visual prompts as feature rectifiers with local proximal constraints to align feature manifolds in non-IID settings, enabling analytical federated learning to achieve competitive accuracy without server-side training costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing one-shot federated learning methods suffer from high server-side computational costs and hyperparameter sensitivity, while analytical federated learning fails under non-IID data due to feature manifold misalignment caused by static feature assumptions.

Method: FedOPAL adapts visual prompts as feature rectifiers, applying local proximal constraints to actively correct heterogeneous feature distributions into a linearly separable space, satisfying the theoretical assumptions of analytical federated learning for gradient-free aggregation.

Result: FedOPAL significantly outperforms original analytical methods on multiple benchmarks and achieves accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art iterative methods while maintaining zero server-side training costs.

Conclusion: FedOPAL provides a new engineering paradigm for efficient large-model collaboration on edge devices by resolving the non-IID data limitation of analytical federated learning without sacrificing server-side efficiency.

Abstract: With the widespread deployment of basic models in edge intelligence, communication bandwidth has become a core bottleneck restricting the scalability of federated learning. Although one-shot federated learning alleviates this problem by minimizing communication rounds, existing iterative fine-tuning or knowledge distillation methods still face challenges such as high server-side computational costs and hyperparameter sensitivity. Analytical federated learning achieves efficient gradientfree aggregation using least-squares closed-form solutions, but in environments with non-independent and identically distributed data, its static feature assumptions fail, leading to feature manifold misalignment and severely impairing model performance. To address this contradiction, this paper proposes the FedOPAL framework. This framework adapts the visual prompts as feature rectifiers, actively correcting the feature distribution of heterogeneous data to a linearly separable space by applying local proximal constraints, thereby satisfying the theoretical assumptions of analytical federated learning. Experimental results show that FedOPAL not only significantly outperforms the original analytical methods on several benchmarks, but also achieves accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art iterative methods while maintaining zero server-side training costs, providing a new engineering paradigm for efficient collaboration of large models on the edge.

</details>


### [225] [Towards Mechanistically Understanding Why Memorized Knowledge Fails to Generalize in Large Language Model Finetuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08393)
*Lu Dai,Ziyang Rao,Yili Wang,Hanqing Wang,Hao Liu,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can memorize new facts during fine-tuning but fail to use them for reasoning due to a "Knowing-Using Gap." Using self-patching, the authors find that knowledge representations exist internally but aren't routed to effective computational layers. A simple heuristic recovers 58-75% of the generalization gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why LLMs memorize new facts during fine-tuning yet fail to apply them in downstream reasoning tasks, formalizing this as the "Knowing-Using Gap."

Method: Formalizes the Knowing-Using Gap with accuracy gap and temporal lag metrics. Introduces self-patching, a novel intervention technique that identifies activation locations where relocating representations improves generalization failures. Tests the knowledge-circuit misalignment hypothesis.

Result: Self-patching reveals that memorized knowledge representations exist internally but are misrouted away from computation-effective layers. A simple heuristic strategy recovers 58-75% of the oracle headroom in generalization failure, with cross-domain robustness.

Conclusion: The Knowing-Using Gap stems from knowledge-circuit misalignment: representations are memorized but not routed to appropriate layers. This diagnosis enables practical mitigation strategies that substantially close the generalization gap.

Abstract: Fine-tuning LLMs to inject new knowledge faces a critical challenge: LLMs can quickly memorize new facts, yet fail to use them for downstream reasoning tasks. We formalize this failure as the \textit{\textbf{Knowing--Using Gap}}, characterized by an accuracy gap and a temporal lag between memorization and generalization. To understand this phenomenon, we fine-tune LLMs with unseen knowledge and monitor the spatial permeation dynamics of the knowledge internally using a novel intervention technique called self-patching. Self-patching identifies activation locations where relocating representations substantially improves failed generalization cases. These results are consistent with a knowledge-circuit misalignment hypothesis: memorized representations can exist internally but may not be routed to computation-effective layers. To demonstrate the practicality of this diagnostic finding, we design a simple heuristic strategy which recovers 58--75\% of the oracle headroom in generalization failure. Experiments are done cross-domain for the robustness of this finding.

</details>


### [226] [Game Theory Driven Multi-Agent Framework Mitigates Language Model Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08403)
*Runzhe Liu,Biquan Bie,Zihao Wang,Yuchao Ma,Yexin Liu,Xinghai Li,Harry Yang,Wenbo Yang,Jinzhe Cao,Shengyang Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: G-Frame, an adaptive multi-agent framework using Bayesian and game theory principles, synthesizes specialized training data to create OmniChem (7B model), which matches GPT-4o mini performance on chemistry benchmarks while reducing hallucinations by 79.46% compared to its base architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lightweight LLMs struggle in rule-based scientific domains due to mimicking linguistic patterns instead of axiomatic reasoning, causing frequent hallucinations that limit their practical application.

Method: G-Frame integrates Bayesian and team game principles into a multi-agent framework that creates an automated closed-loop for high-quality data synthesis. It synthesized 363,045 chains-of-thought and 199,589 question-answer pairs to train the 7B OmniChem model.

Result: OmniChem (7B) achieves performance parity with GPT-4o mini on custom benchmarks and ChemBench, with a 79.46% reduction in hallucinations relative to its base architecture, and demonstrates advanced capabilities in molecular design and synthesis planning.

Conclusion: The work establishes a scalable paradigm using adaptive multi-agents to overcome inherent reasoning deficiencies in LLMs, offering a feasible pathway for accelerating knowledge discovery in specialized scientific fields.

Abstract: The application of lightweight Large Language Models in rule-based scientific domains remains severely limited by their tendency to mimic linguistic patterns rather than reproduce axiomatic reasoning, causing frequent hallucinations. Here, we show that G-Frame, an adaptive multi-agent framework integrating Bayesian and team game principles, establishes an automated closed-loop for high-quality data synthesis and model training. By forcing the internalization of domain constraints through structured reasoning, we synthesized a specialized corpus of 363,045 chains-of-thought and 199,589 question-answer pairs. The resulting 7B model OmniChem achieves performance parity with GPT 4o mini on custom benchmarks and ChemBench while exhibiting a 79.46% reduction in hallucinations relative to its base architecture. We further demonstrate the advanced capabilities of OmniChem in molecular design and synthesis planning. This work establishes a scalable paradigm utilizing adaptive multi-agents to overcome inherent reasoning deficiencies, offering a feasible pathway for accelerating knowledge discovery in specialized scientific fields.

</details>


### [227] [OmniFood-Bench: Evaluating VLMs for Nutrient Reasoning and Personalized Health Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08423)
*Qian Jiang,Zhecheng Shi,Jingpu Yang,Zirui Song,Miao Fang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniFood-Bench is a new benchmark evaluating VLMs on food-related tasks spanning basic perception, quantitative reasoning, and safety-critical advisory. Experiments reveal a "Semantic-Physical Gap": models excel at dish naming but fail catastrophically at mass estimation and generate unsafe advice for diabetic patients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks focus on coarse-grained food classification, failing to evaluate the full reasoning chain required for real-world dietary management — from hidden ingredient identification to mass estimation and safety-critical medical advice synthesis. There is a need to assess VLM trustworthiness in public health domains.

Method: OmniFood-Bench is constructed from the MM-Food-100K dataset and evaluates VLMs across three progressive capabilities: Basic Perception (Ingredients & Cooking Methods), Quantitative Reasoning (Portion Size & Nutritional Profiling), and Safety-Critical Advisory (Disease-Specific Recommendations). Six state-of-the-art VLMs including gpt-5.1, gemini-3-flash, and qwen3-vl-8B are evaluated.

Result: Models achieve near-human accuracy in dish naming but exhibit catastrophic failure in mass estimation and frequently hallucinate benign advice for high-risk diabetic profiles, revealing a "Semantic-Physical Gap".

Conclusion: This work establishes a rigorous standard for evaluating trustworthiness in autonomous agents deployed for public health, highlighting critical deficiencies in VLMs' quantitative reasoning and safety-critical advisory capabilities.

Abstract: The rapid integration of Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) into critical infrastructure promises to revolutionize
  personalized healthcare and dietary management. However, in the domain of food systems, autonomous agents face a
  unique and persistent challenge: the "Systemic Information Asymmetry" between visual appearance and intrinsic
  nutritional composition. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on coarse-grained classification tasks, such as food
  category recognition, which fail to evaluate the intricate reasoning chain required for real-world dietary management
  -- specifically, the ability to traverse from identifying hidden ingredients to estimating physical mass, and finally
  synthesizing safety-critical medical advice. In this paper, we introduce OmniFood-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark
  constructed from the MM-Food-100K dataset. Unlike previous works, OmniFood-Bench evaluates VLMs across three
  progressive capabilities: Basic Perception (Ingredients & Cooking Methods), Quantitative Reasoning (Portion Size &
  Nutritional Profiling), and Safety-Critical Advisory (Disease-Specific Recommendations). We evaluate six
  state-of-the-art VLMs, including gpt-5.1, gemini-3-flash, and qwen3-vl-8B. Our extensive experiments reveal a
  startling "Semantic-Physical Gap": while models achieve near-human accuracy in naming dishes, they exhibit
  catastrophic failure in mass estimation and frequently hallucinate benign advice for high-risk diabetic profiles. This
  work establishes a rigorous standard for trustworthiness in autonomous agents deployed for public health. The code
  and datasets are available in: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/OmniFood-Bench-7D0B

</details>


### [228] [Applying JEPA-Style Predictive Learning to JA4-Derived Network Fingerprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08465)
*Javier Izquierdo,Aygul Zagidullina*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JA4-JEPA applies JEPA-style self-supervised learning to JA4 network fingerprints, achieving strong embedding quality (cosine similarity 0.9899, kNN accuracy 0.9220) on protocol-family classification across TLS, DNS, and SSH.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether the JEPA objective (latent prediction matching) that works well for images and video also generalizes to compact network fingerprints like JA4.

Method: A Transformer-based model (JA4-JEPA) trained on JA4, JA4H, JA4S, and JA4X subfields from JA4DB and CIC-IDS-2017 (~397K samples), using JEPA-style predictive learning where latent predictions are matched to target encoder outputs instead of reconstructing raw input.

Result: On 39,416 held-out samples, the model achieved cosine similarity of 0.9899 and kNN accuracy of 0.9220 for protocol-family classification.

Conclusion: JEPA-style predictive learning produces useful embeddings from JA4-derived fingerprints even with incomplete view overlap across data sources.

Abstract: I-JEPA and V-JEPA learn by matching latent predictions to target encoder outputs rather than regenerating the original input, and this has worked well for images and video. We explore whether the same objective works for compact network fingerprints. We built JA4-JEPA, a Transformer-based model trained on JA4, JA4H, JA4S, and JA4X subfields drawn from JA4DB and CIC-IDS- 2017. The training data combines roughly 397K samples from both sources, though no single sample contains all four view families. We evaluated the learned representations with a frozen kNN probe on protocol-family classification across TLS, DNS, and SSH. On 39,416 heldout samples the model achieved a cosine similarity of 0.9899 and a kNN accuracy of 0.9220. These results indicate that JEPA-style predictive learning can produce useful embeddings from JA4-derived fingerprints, even with incomplete view overlap across sources.
  Keywords: JA4, network fingerprinting, JEPA, predictive representation learning, self-supervised learning

</details>


### [229] [Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR) for Adaptive Semantic Modeling in Biomedical Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08490)
*Bharathwaj Vijayakumar,Sahana K. Varadaraju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DATGR is a lightweight framework that dynamically rewires co-occurrence graph edges using a logistic update rule to model semantic drift in evolving biomedical text, achieving improved AUROC (0.699 vs 0.633) on the BIOMRC corpus while maintaining AUPRC and computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical language evolves rapidly, causing static embeddings and co-occurrence graphs to lose semantic fidelity over time, which degrades performance in retrieval and knowledge discovery tasks.

Method: The Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR) framework dynamically updates co-occurrence edges based on estimated semantic drift using a lightweight, feedback-driven logistic update rule applied to edge weights, avoiding full embedding retraining per time slice.

Result: On BIOMRC, DATGR achieved a mean AUROC improvement of ~0.066 (0.699 vs. 0.633) over a static baseline, with AUPRC remaining comparable (0.738 vs. 0.744), indicating enhanced link-prediction recall without precision loss.

Conclusion: Edge-level adaptation via drift-aware rewiring effectively captures temporal semantic change in evolving biomedical text while remaining computationally efficient and interpretable.

Abstract: Biomedical language evolves rapidly as new discoveries emerge, causing traditional text models to lose semantic fidelity over time. Static embeddings and co-occurrence graphs cannot capture such evolution, leading to performance degradation in retrieval and knowledge discovery tasks. This paper introduces a Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR) framework that models concept evolution by dynamically updating co-occurrence edges based on estimated semantic drift. Instead of retraining embeddings for each time slice, DATGR performs lightweight, feedback-driven rewiring using a logistic update rule applied to edge weights. Evaluated on the Biomedical Multi-Relation Corpus (BIOMRC), the method achieved a mean Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic (AUROC) improvement of approximately 0.066 absolute difference (0.699 vs. 0.633) over a static baseline. Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC) remained comparable (0.738 vs. 0.744), showing that drift-aware adaptation enhances link-prediction recall without a loss in precision. These results demonstrate that edge-level adaptation effectively captures temporal semantic change in evolving biomedical text while remaining computationally efficient and interpretable.

</details>


### [230] [AI-guided stimuli discovery and generation to optimize facial emotion perception studies in autism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08533)
*Kushin Mukherjee,Na Yeon Kim,Maren Wehrheim,Ralph Adolphs,Kohitij Kar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper develops a model-guided framework using artificial neural networks to identify specific facial expressions that reveal autistic-neurotypical perceptual differences, then uses generative adversarial networks to transform these images toward greater perceptual agreement between groups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior studies on facial emotion perception differences between autistic and neurotypical adults show inconsistent findings, likely because group differences are concentrated in a small subset of stimuli (image-level sparsity) rather than uniformly distributed. The authors aim to develop a more sensitive, reliable, and mechanistically informative behavioral assay that can identify the specific conditions under which perceptual differences emerge.

Method: The authors trained population-specific artificial neural network models to predict image-level emotion judgments for autistic and neurotypical participants. These models were used to select novel faces predicted to maximize group separation. In an independent cohort, model-selected images were tested against matched random images. Additionally, a generative adversarial network transformed diagnostic images toward greater predicted group agreement, and synthesized images were validated in a phenotype-matched cohort.

Result: Model-selected images produced larger behavioral differences between autistic and neurotypical groups compared to matched random images in an independent cohort. Synthesized images generated by the GAN reduced behavioral separation relative to their matched original images in phenotype-matched validation.

Conclusion: The study establishes a model-guided framework for discovering and transforming stimuli that reveal population-specific perceptual differences, demonstrating how behavioral phenotyping can move beyond averaging across fixed stimulus sets toward optimized assays that identify when neurodivergent perception diverges or converges.

Abstract: Understanding perceptual differences between autistic and neurotypical adults requires behavioral assays that are sensitive, reliable, and mechanistically informative. Facial emotion perception is a useful test case because group differences have been reported, but findings vary across studies. Here we show that this variability may reflect image-level sparsity: autistic-neurotypical differences in emotion judgments were concentrated in a small subset of diagnostic facial expressions rather than spread uniformly across stimuli. We trained population-specific artificial neural network models to predict image-level judgments for autistic and neurotypical participants, then used these models to select novel faces predicted to maximize group separation. In an independent cohort, model-selected images produced larger behavioral differences than matched random images. We then used the same models with a generative adversarial network to transform diagnostic images toward greater predicted group agreement. In phenotype-matched validation, synthesized images reduced behavioral separation relative to their matched originals. These results establish a model-guided framework for discovering and transforming stimuli that reveal population-specific perceptual differences. More broadly, they show how behavioral phenotyping can move beyond averaging across fixed stimulus sets toward optimized assays that identify the conditions under which neurodivergent perception diverges or converges.

</details>


### [231] [CommuniWave:A Machine Learning Model for Quantifying the Degree of Temporary Informal Behavior in Urban Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08554)
*Hongye Yang,Shien Liu,Zhihao Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CommuniWave is a machine learning model that detects and quantifies informal behaviors in urban communities from street videos, generating Degree of Informal Behavior (DIB) fluctuation charts to support dynamic monitoring and resilience-oriented urban management decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban communities face complexity and uncertainty, yet current top-down planning lacks metrics for quantifying informal resident behaviors, causing conflicts with original plans and hindering territorial resilience.

Method: CommuniWave integrates three components: (1) Behavior Capture Net (BCN) based on mmaction2 for behavior detection, (2) a self-developed YOLOv10 model (YLX) for object detection, and (3) Behavior Eval Model (BEM) using random forest for evaluation. The system processes street videos to generate DIB fluctuation charts.

Result: The model generates Degree of Informal Behavior (DIB) fluctuation charts from street videos, enabling dynamic monitoring of informal behaviors in urban communities.

Conclusion: CommuniWave facilitates refined decision-making for urban managers, supporting the enhancement of overall community resilience through data-driven quantification of informal behaviors.

Abstract: For urban managers and designers, improving the functional attributes of urban communities to enhance territorial resilience in the face of complexity and uncertainty is crucial. Currently, community planning often follows a top-down approach and lacks effective metrics to quantify informal behaviors of residents, leading to frequent conflicts with original plans. This study introduces CommuniWave, a machine learning model designed to efficiently detect and quantify the Degree of Informal Behavior (DIB) in urban communities. The model integrates a Behavior Capture Net (BCN) based on mmaction2, a self-developed YOLOv10 model (YLX), and a Behavior Eval Model (BEM) using random forest. Ultimately, by generating DIB fluctuation charts from street videos, the model facilitates dynamic monitoring, supporting urban managers in making refined decisions to enhance the overall resilience of communities.

</details>


### [232] [SHAP-Weighted Cross-Modal Expert Fusion for Emotion and Sentiment Recognition: Evidence and Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08573)
*Adis Alihodzic,Selma Skopljakovic Hubljar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper revisits XAI-guided adaptive fusion (XGAF) for multimodal emotion/sentiment recognition, analyzing how SHAP attribution reduction methods (mean-abs, median-abs, sum-abs) affect performance when experts have unequal feature dimensionalities. Sum-abs reduction preserves total attribution mass and nearly matches or slightly exceeds early fusion on MELD and CMU-MOSEI benchmarks, while significantly outperforming late fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the trade-off between early fusion (accurate but monolithic) and late fusion (modular but losing cross-modal interactions) by revisiting XAI-guided adaptive fusion, specifically analyzing the effect of SHAP attribution reduction methods when experts have unequal feature dimensionalities.

Method: XGAF uses a tree-based mixture of unimodal and cross-modal experts with sample-level weights derived from TreeSHAP attribution magnitudes. The study compares three SHAP reduction strategies (mean-abs, median-abs, sum-abs) across three face-sequence aggregators on MELD and CMU-MOSEI, with McNemar significance testing and ablation studies.

Result: On MELD, sum-abs XGAF with Transformer reaches 0.5983 WF1 vs early fusion 0.6018 and late fusion 0.4598; McNemar test shows no significant difference from early fusion (p=1.000) but significant superiority over late fusion (p<0.0001). On CMU-MOSEI, sum-abs XGAF reaches 0.6519 WF1, slightly exceeding early fusion (0.6485) and late fusion (0.5696). Mean-abs and median-abs produce nearly uniform weights, while sum-abs concentrates on the trimodal expert.

Conclusion: The main contribution is a transparent empirical analysis showing that SHAP reduction choice (sum-abs preserves total attribution mass), expert dimensionality, and cross-modal expert design critically affect modular multimodal fusion. The primary gain comes from adding cross-modal (especially trimodal) experts rather than complex per-sample routing.

Abstract: Multimodal emotion and sentiment recognition is commonly addressed by early fusion, which concatenates modalities before classification, or late fusion, which combines independently trained unimodal predictors. Early fusion can be accurate but monolithic, while late fusion is modular but may lose cross-modal interactions. This paper revisits XAI-guided adaptive fusion (\xgaf), a tree-based mixture of unimodal and cross-modal experts whose sample-level weights are derived from TreeSHAP attribution magnitudes. We focus on the effect of SHAP attribution reduction when experts have unequal feature dimensionalities. In this setting, mean-abs and median-abs reductions can suppress high-dimensional cross-modal experts, whereas sum-abs reduction preserves total attribution mass. On MELD 7-class emotion recognition, sum-abs \xgaf{} nearly matches early fusion across three face-sequence aggregators; the Transformer variant reaches 0.5983 \wf{}, compared with 0.6018 for early fusion and 0.4598 for probability-average late fusion. McNemar testing shows no significant difference between sum-abs \xgaf{} and early fusion on MELD ($p=1.000$), while \xgaf{} remains significantly better than late fusion ($p<0.0001$). On CMU-MOSEI 3-class sentiment recognition, sum-abs \xgaf{} reaches 0.6519 \wf{}, slightly exceeding early fusion (0.6485) and late fusion (0.5696). Ablation studies show that the main gain comes from adding cross-modal experts, especially the trimodal expert, rather than from complex per-sample routing. Diagnostics further show that mean-abs and median-abs weights are nearly uniform, while sum-abs weights concentrate on the trimodal expert. Thus, the main contribution is a transparent empirical analysis of how SHAP reduction, expert dimensionality, and cross-modal expert design affect modular multimodal fusion.

</details>


### [233] [Towards Precision Therapy in Hepatocellular Carcinoma: A Clinical-Reasoning LLM for Risk Stratification and Treatment Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08602)
*Peng Cui,Jitao Wang,Siyan Xue,Yao Huang,Haoming Xia,Dong Li,Dengxiang Liu,Weilin Wang,Liping Liu,Leida Zhang,Yunfu Cui,Tao Peng,Daolin Ji,Haitao Zhao,Wei Zhang,Xiaojuan Wang,Weijie Ma,Zongren Ding,Jinlong Li,Yuan Ding,Jiajing Zhao,Zhiyu Chen,Chengkun Yang,Ziyue Huang,Jiaqi Liu,Fusheng Liu,Yang Zhou,Xiaojuan Wang,Zhongquan Sun,Shiyun Bao,Xiaojun Wang,Ming Yang,Guangxin Li,Bin Shu,Yong Liao,Hongxuan Li,Yao Tang,Shizhong Yang,Yongyi Zeng,Yufeng Yuan,Yinpeng Dong,Jihui Hao,Jun Zhu,Jiahong Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HCC-STAR is a clinically aligned large language model that reads EMR narratives to jointly perform HCC staging, treatment recommendation with evidence-based rationale, and survival prediction, achieving state-of-the-art performance across a multi-center cohort of 6,668 patients and outperforming clinical guidelines and models including GPT-5 and Gemini-2.5 Pro.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current HCC staging systems (BCLC, CNLC) offer coarse categories that fail to capture within-stage heterogeneity and clinical context embedded in EMRs, limiting precision in risk stratification and treatment decision-making.

Method: Curated ~30,000 HCC cases from SEER, expanded into EMR-style narratives via clinician-validated prompt-based augmentation; developed a knowledge-aligned reasoning framework with step-verifiable composite reward for training, moving beyond text-level memorization of guidelines.

Result: On 6,668 patients from 12 Chinese hospitals, HCC-STAR achieved SOTA in treatment recommendation and risk stratification vs. guidelines, GPT-5, and Gemini-2.5 Pro. Adherence to its recommendations yielded 51-month median survival vs. 29 (BCLC) and 32 (CNLC) months. Blinded specialists rated it trustworthy; it outperformed residents/attendings in treatment accuracy and improved physician decision speed and accuracy as an assistant.

Conclusion: HCC-STAR is a reliable, verifiable decision-support system for risk stratification and precision therapy in HCC, with strong clinical alignment and generalizability.

Abstract: Hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) is a common malignancy and a leading cause of cancer-related mortality. Current guidelines and staging systems provide coarse categories, but often miss within-stage heterogeneity and the clinical context in electronic medical records (EMRs). We present HCC-STAR (Hepatocellular Carcinoma Staging, Treatment And pRognosis), a clinically aligned large language model that reads routine EMR narratives and jointly outputs risk score-based staging, ranked guideline-consistent treatments with evidence-based rationales, and individualized survival estimates. We curated about 30,000 HCC cases from SEER and expanded them into EMR-style narrative training data using a clinician-validated, prompt-based augmentation workflow. On this corpus, we developed a knowledge-aligned reasoning framework optimized with a step-verifiable composite reward, moving beyond text-level memorization of clinical guidelines. In a multi-center cohort of 6,668 patients from 12 hospitals in China, HCC-STAR achieved state-of-the-art performance in treatment recommendation and risk stratification compared with clinical guidelines and competitive models, including GPT-5 and Gemini-2.5 Pro. Hypothetical overall-survival analysis showed a median survival of 51 months under adherence to HCC-STAR recommendations, compared with 29 and 32 months under BCLC and CNLC. In clinician-centric evaluations, blinded hepatobiliary specialists rated HCC-STAR's reasoning and evidence-based justifications as trustworthy. The model surpassed resident and attending physicians in treatment accuracy and helped physicians make more accurate decisions faster when used as an assistant. These findings support HCC-STAR as a reliable and verifiable decision-support system for risk stratification and precision therapy in HCC.

</details>


### [234] [The complexities of patient-centred conversational artificial intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08625)
*João Matos,Olivia Buege,Donny Cheung,Gary S. Collins,Paula Dhiman,Nan Li,Bingyu Mao,Benjamin W. Nelson,Michail Ouroutzoglou,Paul Varghese,Jonathan Amar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Real patient-chatbot conversations (2,053) reveal diverse communication patterns and emotions. A patient simulator modeling clinical content, emotion, conversational strategy, and style produced conversations nearly indistinguishable from real ones (55% human accuracy). Testing four LLMs on urgency assessment across five patient personae (1,164 clinician-graded cases) showed communication style significantly alters triage outcomes, highlighting that idealized chatbots risk underperformance and health disparities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-powered health chatbots are developed and evaluated using cooperative, articulate simulated patients, failing to account for real-world communication diversity. This gap may lead to underperformance and amplified health disparities in actual deployment.
