Method: Analyzed 2,053 real patient-chatbot conversations; built a patient simulator separately modeling clinical content, emotional state, conversational strategy, and communication style; conducted a Turing-inspired realism evaluation with 15 human graders; tested four LLMs on urgency assessment across five patient personae with 1,164 clinician-graded cases.

Result: Simulated conversations were nearly indistinguishable from real ones (human graders achieved 55% accuracy). Communication style significantly altered triage outcomes across different patient personae.

Conclusion: Patient-centered conversational AI must accommodate communication diversity; systems designed for idealized interactions risk underperformance and amplifying health disparities in real-world deployment.

Abstract: Consumer-facing health chatbots powered by large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for symptom assessment. However, chatbot development and evaluation often rely on cooperative, articulate, simulated patients. We analysed 2,053 real patient-chatbot conversations and found that communication patterns and expression of emotions vary widely across users. We developed a patient simulator that separately models clinical content, emotional state, conversational strategy, and communication style. In a Turing-inspired evaluation of realism with 15 human graders, simulated conversations were nearly indistinguishable from real ones, with human graders achieving an accuracy of 55%. We used five distinct patient personae, across 1,164 clinician-graded cases, to evaluate the performance of four LLMs in urgency assessment. We found that communication style can significantly alter triage outcomes. Patient-centred conversational artificial intelligence must accommodate communication diversity: systems designed for idealised, rather than realistic, interactions risk underperforming and amplifying health disparities when deployed in the real world.

</details>


### [235] [Formal Mechanisms for Market Stability in Self-Interested Agent Societies: A Marketplace Simulation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08652)
*Eugene Ng Yi Sheng,Bingquan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how formal mechanisms layered on communication can maintain cooperation in multi-agent LLM marketplaces under adversarial attack. Using 18 DeepSeek-V3 agents in a simulated social dilemma, Mediation emerges as the most robust mechanism, withstanding optimized troll attacks (13.3% utility reduction) without market collapse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-interested agents tend toward defection in repeated social dilemmas, causing collapse of cooperative gains. The paper seeks to identify formal mechanisms that can maintain market stability despite adversarial attacks.

Method: Multi-agent marketplace simulation with 18 DeepSeek-V3 LLM agents possessing complementary production specialties trading in a constrained social network. Two phases: (1) mechanism comparison across eight conditions under progressive troll injection over 200 rounds; (2) adversarial red-teaming of the best mechanism using iteratively prompt-optimized LLM-driven trolls.

Result: Mediation identified as top-performing mechanism. The best adversarial attack (v6) reduced honest-agent utility by 13.3% but could not collapse the market. Mediation enables recovery even under sustained adversarial pressure.

Conclusion: Mediation is adversarially robust: it can be bent but not broken, sustaining positive honest-agent utility under optimized attack.

Abstract: Self-interested agents, left unconstrained, tend toward defection in repeated social dilemmas, causing cooperative gains from trade to collapse. This paper investigates what formal mechanisms, layered on top of unrestricted communication, are sufficient for a society of such agents to maintain market stability, and how resilient those mechanisms are to adversarial attack. We instantiate the research question as a multi-agent marketplace simulation where 18 LLM agents (DeepSeek-V3) with complementary production specialties must trade within a constrained social network to obtain utility. We conduct two experimental phases: (1) a mechanism comparison across eight conditions under progressive troll injection over 200 rounds, identifying Mediation as the top-performing mechanism; and (2) adversarial red-teaming of Mediation using iteratively prompt-optimised LLM-driven trolls, finding that the best attack (v6) reduces honest-agent utility by 13.3% but cannot collapse the market. Mediation enables recovery even under sustained adversarial pressure. We define adversarial robustness as a mechanism's ability to sustain positive honest-agent utility under optimised attack, and find that Mediation is robust: it can be bent but not broken.

</details>


### [236] [SolarChain-Eval: A Physics-Constrained Benchmark for Trustworthy Economic Agents in Decentralized Energy Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08681)
*Shilin Ou,Yifan Xu,Luyao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SolarChain-Eval is a physics-constrained benchmark for evaluating trustworthy AI agents in decentralized energy markets, revealing a utility-safety trade-off where RL agents improve market utility but can exploit invalid data, while LLM-based Planner/Auditor layers improve auditability but cannot fully compensate for misspecified reward functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agentic AI systems are deployed in cyber-physical environments (e.g., decentralized energy markets), evaluation must assess both task performance and trustworthiness, since autonomous agents may exploit invalid physical data, create artificial liquidity, and produce unstable governance decisions.

Method: SolarChain-Eval formulates market governance as a Gymnasium-compatible MDP with hourly decisions, evaluates policies across market utility, physical safety, slippage, action smoothness, spatial fairness, and auditability dimensions, and incorporates an LLM-based Planner/Auditor layer that defines action bounds and audit rules while recording structured intervention logs.

Result: Experiments with static, random, myopic, RL, and RL+LLM policies show RL agents improve market utility but can produce unsafe behavior; removing physics penalty leads to exploitation of invalid generation and artificial liquidity; LLM Planner/Auditor improves auditability and mitigates selected risks but cannot fully compensate for misspecified reward functions.

Conclusion: Trustworthy agentic AI evaluation requires both physical constraints and transparent intervention traces; a utility-safety trade-off is inherent; LLM-based auditing is helpful but insufficient if the reward function itself is misspecified.

Abstract: As agentic AI systems are increasingly applied to cyber-physical environments, their evaluation requires assessment of both task performance and trustworthiness. In decentralized energy markets, autonomous agents may improve market utility, but may also exploit invalid physical data, create artificial liquidity, and produce unstable governance decisions. Therefore, we propose SolarChain-Eval, a physics-constrained benchmark for evaluating trustworthy economic agents. It formulates market governance as a Gymnasium-compatible Markov Decision Process, where agents make hourly decisions. SolarChain-Eval evaluates each policy across multiple dimensions, including market utility, physical safety, slippage, action smoothness, spatial fairness, and auditability. To support agentic evaluation, SolarChain-Eval incorporates an LLM-based Planner/Auditor layer. The Planner defines episode-level action bounds and audit rules, while the Auditor reviews and revises high-risk actions. All interventions are recorded through structured logs, including trigger signals, proposed actions, revised actions, and audit rationales. Experiments with static, random, myopic, RL, and RL+LLM policies reveal a clear utility-safety trade-off. RL agents improve market utility but can still produce unsafe behavior. When the physics penalty is removed, reward-maximizing agents exploit invalid generation and increase artificial liquidity. The LLM Planner/Auditor improves auditability and mitigates selected risks, but it cannot fully compensate for a misspecified reward function. These results indicate that trustworthy agentic AI evaluation requires both physical constraints and transparent intervention traces. We release data and code as open access on GitHub for replicability.

</details>


### [237] [Remember When It Matters: Proactive Memory Agent for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08716)
*Yifan Wu,Lizhu Zhang,Yuhang Zhou,Mingyi Wang,Bo Peng,Serena Li,Xiangjun Fan,Zhuokai Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "behavioral state decay" in long-horizon tasks where decision-relevant information gets lost in expanding trajectories. It proposes a separate memory agent that actively updates a structured memory bank and selectively injects reminders into an unmodified action agent. This plug-and-play module improves pass@1 by +8.3 pp on Terminal-Bench and +6.8 pp on τ²-Bench, with selective intervention outperforming passive or always-on baselines. They also train an open-weight memory policy (Qwen3.5-27B) using SFT and GRPO on SETA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address "behavioral state decay," where decision-relevant state (task requirements, environment facts, prior attempts, etc.) gets scattered or lost across long trajectories, causing failures in decision-making.

Method: A separate memory agent runs alongside an unmodified action agent. It updates a structured memory bank from the recent trajectory and decides whether to inject a memory-grounded reminder or remain silent. The module is plug-and-play with frontier action agents and existing agent harnesses. Open-weight memory policies are trained via SFT and GRPO on SETA.

Result: Improves pass@1 for both weaker and stronger action agents: +8.3 pp on Terminal-Bench 2.0 and +6.8 pp on τ²-Bench. Selective intervention outperforms passive bank exposure, always-on injection, advisor-only guidance, and general retrieval. Training Qwen3.5-27B on SETA improves validation reward and achieves partial transfer to Terminal-Bench.

Conclusion: Active, selective memory intervention is more effective than passive or always-on memory mechanisms for mitigating behavioral state decay in long-horizon tasks, and open-weight memory policies show promising transfer capabilities.

Abstract: In long-horizon tasks, decision-relevant state is often scattered across an expanding trajectory, while the action agent must surface it and act. As trajectories grow, task requirements, environment facts, prior attempts, diagnoses, and open subgoals can be buried in the context window or pushed beyond it, failing to influence decisions when needed. We call this failure mode "behavioral state decay". We study memory as an active intervention mechanism rather than passive retrieval. A separate memory agent runs alongside an unmodified action agent, updating a structured memory bank from the recent trajectory and deciding whether to inject a memory-grounded reminder or remain silent. The module is plug-and-play with frontier action agents and existing agent harnesses. Across Terminal-Bench 2.0 and $τ^2$-Bench, it improves pass@1 for both weaker and stronger action agents, with gains of +8.3 pp on Terminal-Bench and +6.8 pp on $τ^2$-Bench. Ablations show that selective intervention outperforms passive bank exposure, always-on injection, advisor-only guidance, and general retrieval. As an early step toward open-weight memory policies, we train Qwen3.5-27B on SETA using SFT and GRPO, improving validation reward and achieving partial transfer to Terminal-Bench.

</details>


### [238] [The Illusion of Equivalency: Statistical Characterization of Quantization Effects in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08734)
*Baha Rababah,Cuneyt Gurcan Akcora,Carson K. Leung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Standard metrics like accuracy and perplexity fail to capture behavioral divergences in quantized LLMs; the paper introduces correctness agreement as a decision-level metric and shows that moderate quantization causes behavioral changes even when task performance seems preserved, with query/key projections being more sensitive than value/output projections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training quantization evaluation relies almost exclusively on accuracy and perplexity, which fail to capture behavioral changes induced by quantization, creating an illusion of equivalence between base and quantized models.

Method: Introduces correctness agreement (a decision-level metric measuring overlap in correct predictions between base and quantized models), analyzes quantization as a structural operator on attention weights, and quantifies layer-wise distortions using statistical and distributional measures across models and quantization schemes from 8-bit to 2-bit.

Result: Behavioral divergence emerges under moderate quantization even when task performance appears preserved; non-linear breakpoints occur at low bit-widths; query and key projections are consistently more sensitive than value and output projections.

Conclusion: Standard metrics create an illusion of equivalence between base and quantized models; behavioral evaluation beyond conventional performance metrics is needed.

Abstract: Post-training quantization is widely used to deploy large language models in resource-constrained settings, yet its evaluation relies almost exclusively on accuracy and perplexity. We show that these metrics fail to capture behavioral changes induced by quantization. We introduce correctness agreement, a decision-level metric that measures overlap in correct predictions between a base model and its quantized variants, independent of absolute accuracy. Across multiple models and quantization schemes from 8-bit to 2-bit, we find that behavioral divergence emerges under moderate quantization even when task performance appears preserved. To explain this effect, we analyze quantization as a structural operator on attention weights and quantify layer-wise distortions using statistical and distributional measures. Our results reveal non-linear breakpoints at low bit-widths and show that query and key projections are consistently more sensitive than value and output projections. These findings expose an illusion of equivalence between base and quantized models and motivate behavioral evaluation beyond conventional performance metrics.

</details>


### [239] [AUTOPILOT VQA: Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Incident-Centric Dashcam Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08745)
*Siddharth Damodharan,Radhika Gupta,Ali Alshami,Ryan Rabinowitz,Jugal Kalita*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AUTOPILOT-VQA is an incident-centric visual question answering benchmark for dashcam video understanding, designed to evaluate vision-language models on safety-critical reasoning through structured questions about real-world driving incidents and near-incidents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Vision-Language Models, Large Language Models, and Multimodal Large Language Models have advanced autonomous driving tasks, but there is no reliable benchmark to evaluate whether these models can reason about safety-critical incidents. The paper aims to address this evaluation gap.

Method: The authors construct a VQA benchmark centered on real-world driving incidents and near-incidents, with structured questions covering diverse safety-relevant categories: weather/lighting, traffic environment, road layout, road surface, signage, entities, accident occurrence, impact location, and avoidability reasoning. The dataset is released as part of the AUTOPILOT CVPR 2026 competition.

Result: The benchmark provides a standardized evaluation framework that moves beyond object recognition toward temporally grounded, safety-aware reasoning for autonomous driving systems.

Conclusion: AUTOPILOT-VQA supports the development of more interpretable, robust, and safety-conscious vision-language systems for real-world autonomous driving by providing a structured, incident-centric evaluation benchmark.

Abstract: Recent advances in Vision-Language Models, Large Language Models, and Multimodal Large Language Models have improved autonomous driving tasks such as scene understanding, decision making, trajectory prediction, and visual question answering. However, evaluating whether these models can reliably reason about safety-critical incidents remains challenging. To address this gap, we present AUTOPILOT-VQA, an incident-centric visual question answering benchmark for dashcam video understanding. The dataset evaluates different systems through structured questions designed around real-world driving incidents and near-incidents. The benchmark covers diverse safety-relevant categories, including weather and lighting conditions, traffic environment, road layout, road surface state, signage, involved entities, accident occurrence, impact location, and avoidability-related reasoning. By requiring models to answer grounded questions about both contextual scene properties and event-level incident details, AUTOPILOT-VQA moves beyond object recognition toward temporally grounded, safety-aware reasoning. The dataset is released as part of the AUTOPILOT CVPR 2026 competition and provides a standardized benchmark for assessing the reliability of autonomous driving systems in different scenarios. Our benchmark support developments for more interpretable, robust, and safety-conscious vision-language systems for real-world autonomous driving.

</details>


### [240] [Using AI-based Learning Assistants in Higher Education: A Large-Scale Descriptive Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08748)
*Kristina Schaaff,Quintus Stierstorfer,Valerie Heckel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Large-scale analysis of 77,543 distance students reveals that Syntea, an AI learning assistant, is embedded in study routines with usage patterns varying across gender, age, study cluster, degree, and study mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on educational chatbots relies on small samples and self-reported data; large-scale evidence on actual usage behavior is lacking.

Method: Descriptive analysis of objective log data from 77,543 distance students examining usage patterns across gender, age group, study cluster, degree, and study mode.

Result: Syntea is embedded in study routines, but usage differs across demographic and structural contexts (gender, age, study cluster, degree, study mode).

Conclusion: The findings provide an empirical basis for further development of AI-based learning support and contribute a large-scale analysis of educational chatbot usage in higher education.

Abstract: In this study, we present a large-scale descriptive analysis of the use of an AI-based learning assistant (Syntea) in higher education. Based on objective log data from 77,543 students enrolled in distance studies, we examine usage patterns across gender, age group, study cluster, degree, and study mode. To date, existing research on educational chatbots has largely relied on comparatively small samples and self-reported survey data, while large-scale evidence on actual usage behavior remains limited. Our findings show that Syntea is already embedded in the study routines of many learners, but that usage differs across demographic and structural contexts. By identifying these patterns, our study provides an empirical basis for the further development of AI-based learning support and contributes a large-scale analysis of educational chatbot usage in higher education.

</details>


### [241] [Ideas Have Genomes: Benchmarking Scientific Lineage Reasoning and Lineage-Grounded Idea Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08758)
*Yifan Zhou,Qihao Yang,Yan Li,Donggang Li,Xiru Hu,Hokin Deng,Ziyang Gong,Xuanyi Zhou,Huacan Wang,Xiangchao Yan,Wanghan Xu,Wenlong Zhang,Shaofeng Zhang,Yue Zhou,Yifan Yang,Zhihang Zhong,Xue Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IdeaGene-Bench evaluates AI systems on scientific lineage reasoning and idea generation across 10 domains, finding that even the strongest LLM achieves only 27.3% accuracy and structured lineage context reshuffles rankings non-uniformly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks do not assess whether AI systems can follow the inheritance structure of scientific ideas (borrowing, fixing, recombining prior work), which is fundamental to how real science progresses.

Method: Built on the IdeaGene framework—papers represented as typed, evidence-grounded Idea Genome objects with GenomeDiff tracking evolutionary dynamics (inheritance, mutation, loss, import, insertion). Includes 1,961 lineage traces, 1,085 Idea Genome objects, 920 GenomeDiff records across 10 domains. Two evaluation modes: IG-Exam (42 task types, 1,029 instances) for closed-form reasoning, and IG-Arena (Population-Evolution Score) for lineage-conditioned generation.

Result: Best LLM achieves 27.3% exact accuracy on lineage reasoning; lineage context reshuffles rankings across models rather than uniformly improving performance.

Conclusion: There is a compositional bottleneck in current LLM-based scientists for scientific lineage reasoning and grounded idea generation; IG-Bench reveals systematic limitations not captured by existing benchmarks.

Abstract: Scientific ideas rarely start from a blank page. They inherit mechanisms, repair known limitations, and recombine pieces of earlier work, much like biological genomes. Current benchmarks still say little about whether AI systems can follow this inheritance structure. We present IdeaGene-Bench (IG-Bench), a benchmark for scientific lineage reasoning and lineage-grounded idea generation. IG-Bench is organized around the IdeaGene framework: each paper or proposal is represented as a set of minimal, typed, evidence-grounded Idea Genome objects, and a GenomeDiff aligns these objects to record inheritance, mutation, loss, external import, and novel insertion under six operational evolutionary dynamics. The benchmark contains 1,961 golden lineage traces, 1,085 curated Idea Genome objects, and 920 pairwise GenomeDiff records across 10 scientific domains. It supports two evaluations. IG-Exam (42 task types, 1,029 instances) tests closed-form lineage reasoning across Idea Genome abstraction, inheritance tracing, evolutionary reasoning, and lineage verification. IG-Arena evaluates generation with a lineage-conditioned Population-Evolution Score(PES), asking whether a proposal can be inserted as a coherent descendant of a given lineage population: it should inherit the right Idea Genome objects, vary meaningfully from nearby work, and offer selection value for future research. Experiments on 14 LLM-based scientists expose a compositional bottleneck. The strongest system reaches only 27.3% exact accuracy on lineage reasoning, and structured lineage context reshuffles system rankings rather than helping every participant uniformly.

</details>
